## Three endings written three times, and a pass over zero tables (2026-08-23)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Output-decision follow-ups", the first, second and fourth entries filed by the run directly below: the script's three stops "are still three hand-written `if`s"; `provenanceOutput` "returns `ok: true` for an empty outcome list and a case documents that rather than refusing it"; and `ProvenanceLine.stream` "is a string union and the script maps it to `console.log`/`console.error` with a ternary". The list-of-checks half of the first entry was rejected on inspection and the script now says why. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-23] [est: 11000] **`stopOn`, `printProvenanceOutput`, and a refusal that does not need its caller** — `scripts/check-privacy-baseline-provenance.ts`, `lib/provenance-report.ts`, 2 cases changed and 1 added in `__tests__/provenance-report.test.ts`.

  **Three copies of an ending.** Three checks stop this guard before it reports anything — the work tree, the registry, the diff's shape — and each was its own four-line block with its own `console.error` and its own `process.exit(1)`. Three copies of an ending is how one of them comes to exit 0, or to print on stdout, in a diff nobody reads twice. `stopOn(refusal)` is the ending, once, and all three now hand it a `string | null` — including the work-tree check, whose `kind: "fail"` becomes a refusal string at the call site so the three read as three of the same thing.

  **The suggestion asked for a LIST of checks and the script says why it is not one.** The diff cannot be asked for before a base revision resolves, so a list would be a list of thunks and the order — the only interesting thing about it — would stop being visible. What the three genuinely share is the ending, and that is what was extracted.

  **A pass over zero tables, refused where the claim lives.** `provenanceOutput` returned `ok: true` for an empty outcome list, because `every` over nothing is true; the thing that stopped that reaching it was `provenanceRegistryRefusal`, one level up, in a caller neither this module nor its suite controls. It now refuses locally and `halted`s, with the registry refusal still in front of it — a second opinion rather than a replacement, and a case says which is which.

  **The last half-sentence of the stream rule moved with it.** The script walked `output.lines` and mapped `"stdout"` to `console.log` itself, which is a caller that can disagree with the rule that assigned the name. `printProvenanceOutput(output, out = console)` takes the console as a parameter so a test can be the console, and the sweep now also refuses `output.lines` in the script.

  Mutation-checked both ways: short-circuiting the empty-run refusal turns its case red, and swapping the two streams in the printer turns the printing case red.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6550 tests (+1), build. No DB migration, no creds.

## The cheapest property with the most expensive cover (2026-08-23)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Order-and-path follow-ups", the top entry: "The script's report loop is pinned by a case that spawns a scratch repository, which is the slowest cover in the suite for the cheapest property ... extracting the 'which reports, in what order, to which stream' decision into a pure function over outcomes would let one call assert it, and would leave the scratch repository for the git questions only it can answer." The second entry of the same group — the `unparseable` case tied to three formatters' wording — is closed in the same pass, because the extraction is what made a derived assertion available. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-23] [est: 16000] **`provenanceOutput` — the guard's output decision, made testable without a repository** — new `lib/provenance-report.ts`, `scripts/check-privacy-baseline-provenance.ts`, 11 cases in `__tests__/provenance-report.test.ts`, one rewritten case in `__tests__/privacy-baseline-provenance-script.test.ts`.

  **Four rules that were statements in a script.** An `unparseable` table stops the run and nothing else prints; reports print in registry order, all of them, before any exit code; each report goes to the stream its own verdict earns; skip lines come last and there is either one for the run or one per table. None of them need git, and all of them were covered only by a case that builds a throwaway repository, commits to it twice and spawns the script. That cover is right for the questions only a repository can answer and it is the wrong altitude for "does the second report print after the first".

  **Text in, lines out.** The function returns `{lines: [{stream, text}], ok, halted}` and the script's print half is now four lines: iterate, pick the stream from the line, exit on `halted`, honour `ok`. A case asserts the script no longer picks a stream from a verdict itself, so rule 3 cannot come to exist in two places.

  **`halted` is a separate bit from `ok`, and the trailing summary is why.** A run stopped by an unparseable table has not read the other tables, so the "that run read history from …" line would be describing something that did not happen. The script exits before it; the flag is what says so out loud.

  **The wording coupling the extraction exposed, closed.** The scratch-repository case asserted that the word "well-formed" appeared nowhere on stdout — true today because three formatters happen to share the word, and a pass over a stdout full of pass lines the day one of them says "valid". It now asserts that a halted run prints nothing on stdout but the redirect announcement, which is derived from the run rather than from a formatter's vocabulary, and which the mutation check confirms is load-bearing in both suites.

  Mutation-checked: short-circuiting the halt rule turns the new suite red AND the scratch-repository case red — the second is the one that would have stayed green under the old wording assertion.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6549 tests (+11), build. No DB migration, no creds.

## The one input every evaluator trusted (2026-08-23)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Closed-set follow-ups", the fourth entry: "Every provenance evaluator validates its inputs and none validates `changedFiles` ... A caller that passed absolute paths, or output from a `git diff` run in a subdirectory, would find nothing in the set and every value would read as unbacked; the guard would report six failures naming files the reader can see in their own diff." Closed together with the "Provenance-registry follow-ups" entry asking for a sentence in `ProvenanceBaseRevision` saying the list is deliberately unfiltered — the same field, and the sentence is half of why the check belongs to the loop rather than to a table. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-23] [est: 12000] **`changedFilesRefusal` — the diff is shape-checked before any table reads it** — `lib/provenance-tables.ts`, `scripts/check-privacy-baseline-provenance.ts`, 6 cases in `__tests__/provenance-tables.test.ts`.

  **Six right-shaped failures with the wrong cause.** Every evaluator validates its own table down to the note's word count, and then compares a recorded path against a list that arrives from `git diff --name-only` and is trusted on sight. A diff run somewhere other than the repository root, or a caller that resolved the paths first, makes every lookup miss: each table reports its value as describing an untouched file, and the reader spends the morning looking for a bug in the recorded values while the answer is in the caller. A stopped run with a reason is worth more than six failures naming files the reader can see in their own diff.

  **A guard-level check, because the list is a guard-level fact.** It is read once and shared by all three tables, so a list that is not repo-relative is wrong for all of them in the same way — one refusal beside `provenanceRegistryRefusal`, not a rule each table has to remember. `ProvenanceBaseRevision.changedFiles` now says out loud that the list is deliberately unfiltered and shared, which is the other half of the same argument.

  **Two shapes refused, one deliberately not.** Absolute paths (posix `/…`, or a `C:` drive prefix) and paths re-anchored with `./` or `../` cannot come out of a repo-root `--name-only` and cannot match a recorded path. Backslashes are left alone and the module says why: git emits forward slashes on Windows too, so a backslash here is either a legal posix filename or one of git's quoted `"…\303\251…"` paths, and a guard that refused to run because somebody added a file with an accent in its name is a guard that gets deleted within the week. An empty list stays legal — it means the diff touched nothing, which each table already reads correctly.

  **The message is the diagnosis.** It counts offenders against the whole list (“1 of 2” and “2 of 2” are different problems), quotes three and says how many it did not, states what the guard would have concluded, and names the two causes that actually produce it. A case pins the truncation, because the failure is systematic and a refusal that printed a 400-path diff would bury its own first sentence.

  **Ordering is the whole value, so it is asserted rather than assumed** — a source case requires the script's call to come before the loop, since a refusal printed under the six failures it exists to prevent is a footnote.

  Mutation-checked both ways: dropping the `../` pattern turns the refusal case red, and moving the script's call after the loop turns the ordering case red.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6538 tests (+6), build. No DB migration, no creds.

The rule is now one module and each table brings the three phrases that genuinely differ.

  **The merge is asserted, not described.** One case renders both refusals, substitutes the three per-table phrases, and requires the strings to be equal — so a hand-written third wording goes red rather than passing as prose nobody diffed. A source sweep requires any evaluator that raises `unknown_language` to import the shared rule, with a positive control that both still raise it.

  **The keyless table now says why it is out of scope** rather than leaving a reader to reconstruct it from three modules: `SCRUB_PROMISE_BASELINE` is one record measured from one constant file, so there is no key to be unknown and the rule would be a check on nothing.

That was the argument for building the registry before a third table existed rather than after, and this is the run that could have disproved it.

  **What it closes.** The promise fingerprint shipped with a static case — edit the clause, the parity suite goes red. That is what it is for, and it is also the shape that decays: the documented repair is "record the new value here", the failure message says so in as many words, and a value whose repair is a paste is one commit from being a rubber stamp. The drift half now asks whether the note MOVED with the number, and whether `PRIVACY.md` — the file the number is measured from — was touched in the same diff.

What is shared is `policyChecksum`, so the two values stay comparable, and the loop, so neither owns a copy of the git half. ## The promise a narrowed word could survive (2026-08-23)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Promise-parity follow-ups", the third entry: "The probe table matches promised phrases by regex and three of the four patterns are anchored on one word ... 'email address' narrowed to 'email domain' still matches /^email/i and would keep the same probe. The exhaustiveness case catches an ADDED field and cannot catch a narrowed one. A checksum of the promise sentence, next to the existing section checksum, is the cheap complement." The second entry of the same group was checked and closed as already shipped — `resolveSentryConfig` cannot enable Sentry in `development`, and three suites already pin it. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-23] [est: 18000] **Fingerprint the sentence the probes were matched to** — `SCRUB_PROMISE_BASELINE` in `lib/sentry-scrub-promises.ts`, six cases in `__tests__/sentry-scrub-policy-parity.test.ts`.

  **The parity suite reads the promise as a LIST and the gap is in the words.** It asks which fields the policy names and whether each is claimed by a probe, which catches a field ADDED to the disclosure with nothing checking it. It cannot catch one NARROWED, because the probe patterns are anchored on a single word each — `/^email/i`, `/^ip\b/i`, `/^authorization/i` — so "email address" reworded to "email domain" still matches, still claims the same probe, and leaves every assertion green while the app and the disclosure have come apart.

  **The checksum cannot read the new wording either, and that is the whole claim.** It refuses to let the rewording pass unlooked-at: any edit to the clause turns the suite red and the documented repair is to re-read every probe against the new sentence, then record what was found. The same reviewability guarantee — and the same honest limit — as the translation checksums next door.

  **Fingerprinted over the CLAUSE, not the paragraph.** From the intro through the period that ends its sentence, normalised first through the same `normalisePolicyText` the translation checksums use, because the policy is hard-wrapped inside a blockquote and a guard that goes red for a rewrap is a guard somebody deletes within a month. A fingerprint over the rest of the policy would move on every unrelated edit, so a clause with no terminator is a throw rather than a slice to end of file.

  **One checksum implementation, not two.** `checksum` in `lib/privacy-translated-section.ts` became the exported `policyChecksum`: a second spelling of "sixteen hex of SHA-256" is how two recorded values stop being comparable, and every provenance value in this repository now comes through one function.

  **The case for the rule is an experiment rather than a sentence in a comment** — one case performs the narrowing, asserts the exhaustiveness check is still satisfied by it, and then asserts the fingerprint moved. If that ever stops being true, the checksum is buying nothing and the case says so instead of the comment.

  **The value carries provenance**, not just a number: `recordedOn` and a note of at least eight words, for the same reason the translation notes ask for eight — this value moves for more than one reason (a rewrap that changed nothing, a narrowed field, a field added) and the note is the only place the diff says which.

  Mutation-checked: rewording "stripped client-side" to "redacted client-side" in `PRIVACY.md` turns the checksum case red by name; a rewrap of the same clause leaves it green.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6492 tests (+6), build. No DB migration, no creds.

## The third copy, written before it existed (2026-08-23)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Two-table-guard follow-ups", the top entry: "One guard now covers two tables with two parsers, two evaluators and two reports, and the only thing they share is the base-ref resolution ... the shape that actually generalises is a list of `{module, parse, evaluate, format}` records the script loops over, and writing it before the third table is how it stays a loop rather than a third copy." -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-23] [est: 28000] **The provenance guard loops over a registry instead of carrying one pass per table** — `lib/provenance-tables.ts` and `__tests__/provenance-tables.test.ts`, with `scripts/check-privacy-baseline-provenance.ts` reduced to the git half.

  **Two copies is a shape; the third is the copy nobody reviews.** The script asked one question of two tables — a recorded measurement moved, did the diff say why — and asked it twice, in two hand-written passes of the same six steps. The tables were already drifting in ways nobody chose: one three-way classification and one two-way, one `git diff --name-only` each for the same diff, two `previous === null` tests spelled differently. The suggestion's own timing argument is the reason this shipped at two rather than at three: written after the third table lands, the loop is a refactor of three copies; written before, it is the thing the third table is added to.

  **What each table now brings, and nothing else:** its module path, its current value, how to parse a previous revision, how to judge one, and the five sentences it says about itself. Adding a table is an entry in `PROVENANCE_TABLES`; the script does not change.

  **The generic parameter is erased at the boundary on purpose.** `ProvenanceTable` is a closure over a table's own type, so the registry holds two entries whose tables share no field name without widening either to `unknown` — and so the script cannot reach a parsed table at all, which is the point: it has nothing left to get wrong about one.

  **What deliberately stayed in the script is everything that needs git** — the base-ref resolution (`--base`, the env var, the PR target, `HEAD~1`, all through `merge-base`), the work-tree probe, the shallow-clone refusal. That is the CI-specific reasoning, it is shared by every table by construction, and it is the half that cannot be unit-tested without a repository. Everything in the new module is pure: text in, verdict and sentences out, which is why the loop's ordering rules are now pinned against a fabricated table rather than against a legal document.

  **The one place the two entries disagree is now declared rather than derived.** The baselines table treats "the module was there and yielded no table" as a hard failure; the translation table collapses it into the same skip an absent module gets, because that parser is days old and the suite round-trips it every run. Before, that difference lived in two functions that happened to be written differently; it is now two lines in one file with the argument beside them, and a case pins each side so closing the gap later is a decision somebody makes out loud.

  **Related bug found by the same pass and fixed in it:** a loop can skip itself in a way two hand-written passes could not — `every` over nothing is true, so a registry that lost its entries would exit 0 with every report line gone and nothing saying why. `provenanceRegistryRefusal` refuses an empty registry and a duplicated id (both entries would run, and every case addressing a table by id reaches only the first), which is the claim each table's own formatter already makes about ITS records — "a pass over zero baselines is not a pass" — made one level up, where the records now live.

  **Behaviour is byte-identical where it should be:** all ten existing script cases pass unchanged, including the two that match on the exact wording of the per-table skip lines. The one behavioural change is that `git diff --name-only` runs once rather than twice.

  Mutation-checked twice: classifying a base revision that does not exist turns one loop case red; flipping the translation table onto the baselines' three-way classification turns two red, one of them the case that exists to say the divergence is deliberate.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6486 tests (+18), build. No DB migration, no creds.

## The table whose repair is a paste (2026-08-22)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Two-checksum follow-ups", the top entry: "The table now holds ten checksums and one script compares any provenance table against its previous revision — this is not the table it compares ... the suite requires a note of at least eight words and cannot see whether the note MOVED with the checksum. That is the whole difference between a record and a ritual." -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-22] [est: 35000] **`PRIVACY_TRANSLATION_SOURCES` gets the provenance guard its neighbour has** — `lib/privacy-translation-provenance.ts`, wired into `scripts/check-privacy-baseline-provenance.ts`.

  **The checksums' strength is also their weakness.** They turn every edit to a shipped legal page into a red suite, and the documented repair is "record the new value here" — the failure message says so in as many words. A table whose repair is a paste is one commit from being a rubber stamp. `PRIVACY_BODY_BASELINES` has the same problem and the same answer next door; this is that answer applied to the second table, not a new idea.

  **What it can and cannot do, stated rather than implied.** It cannot tell a re-translation from a paste — nothing can, both change the file and both change the checksum. It forces the diff to SAY which, to a reviewer who may not read the language. A reviewability guarantee, which is the only kind available for prose.

  **One deliberate divergence from the rule it copies.** The baselines drift requires the note to NAME the number it replaced, because "1171 → 980" is a fact a reader can weigh. Sixteen hex characters are not: a note quoting the old checksum proves only that somebody could copy. What is required here is that the note MOVED, which is what a paste does not do — and the minimum length is eight words rather than five, because a checksum moves for two reasons (the English changed, or this page did) and the note is the only place the diff says which.

  **Which file has to be in the diff depends on which checksum moved**, and that is the useful half of the message: `sourceChecksum` is measured from `PRIVACY.md`, `translatedChecksum` from that language's own page. A value that moved with its file untouched cannot have been measured from anything.

  **One guard, two tables, two independent verdicts.** Sharing the script rather than adding a second one, because the base-ref resolution — `--base`, the env var, the PR target, `HEAD~1`, all through `merge-base` — is where the CI-specific reasoning lives and is the part a second script would copy. Both reports print before either exit code is honoured, since the commit that amends the English disclosure moves the word count and all five checksums; each report goes to the stream its own verdict earns.

  **Two existing script cases matched on output this change reworded, and both were fixed by making the output more specific rather than the assertions looser** — the translation skip line no longer says "drift half skipped" (two skips with different modules behind them is how somebody concludes the whole guard sat out), and the redirected-history line says "pass" only when it was one.

  Mutation-checked end to end: a base revision whose checksums differ while the note and date match the shipped ones exits 1, naming `de` and "same note and date", with the other table's pass still on stdout.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6468 tests (+17), build.

## The promise and the code that keeps it (2026-08-22)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Two-checksum follow-ups" — the last of the five, taken ahead of the four above it because it is the only one where the failure is the app doing something different from what it told a user: "A rule tying the scrub list in lib/sentry-config.ts to the field names in PRIVACY.md would close the one gap where the app could stop doing what the policy promises." (The scrub list is in `lib/sentry.ts`, not `lib/sentry-config.ts` — the suggestion named the wrong file.) The first entry, generalising the provenance script over the new checksum table, is left at the top of the file for the next run. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-22] [est: 20000] **`scrubPII` against the sentence that promises it** — `lib/sentry-scrub-promises.ts` and `__tests__/sentry-scrub-policy-parity.test.ts`.

  **Two well-covered files and nothing between them.** `PRIVACY.md` promises "personally identifying fields (email address, IP address, cookies, and `Authorization` headers) are stripped client-side before transmission"; `sentry-scrubber.test.ts` checks each field `scrubPII` removes. Neither asks whether those are the same fields, and each file stays internally consistent while the pair comes apart.

  **One-directional, and that is the whole framing.** Stripping more than the policy mentions is the app doing better than it said. Promising more than the scrubber removes is the app sending a third party data it told a user it would not — and it arrives by ordinary editing, not by malice.

  **The promise is PARSED out of the policy, not restated.** A hand-kept list of the four fields would be a third copy of the sentence and would drift exactly the way this rule exists to prevent. What is hand-kept is the probe per field — a value that must not survive, and where in a Sentry event it sits — which is the part no parser can infer, and it is held exhaustive against the sentence in both directions: a promised field no probe claims, and a probe matching nothing the policy still promises.

  **The other half of the promise gets a case too.** The disclosure says the payload DOES carry the Supabase user identifier so reports correlate per account; a scrubber that stripped everything would satisfy every case above and break what the policy says the product does.

  Mutation-checked twice: deleting `delete headers.Authorization` from `lib/sentry.ts` fails the Bearer probe with "the app sending a third party something it told a user it would not"; adding "device identifiers" to the policy's list fails the exhaustiveness case by name, because nothing would ever have checked it.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6451 tests (+9), build.

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Completeness-rule follow-ups": "`PRIVACY.md` has six translations as separate files and no coverage rule touches them at all ... the same defect class the whole i18n programme just closed, one directory over, and the honest unit there is a per-file checksum of the English source recorded next to each translation." - [DONE: 2026-08-22] [est: 30000] **A checksum of the English disclosure, recorded next to each translation** — `lib/privacy-translated-section.ts` and `__tests__/privacy-translated-section.test.ts`.

  **The five translated pages are not translations of `PRIVACY.md`, which is why nothing had noticed.** Each carries ONE section — the "Crash reporting and diagnostics" blockquote naming Sentry — published under GDPR art. 12 for a reader who cannot read the full policy. Every rule the repo had about them is a property of the TRANSLATED file: a word count floored against its own baseline, a similarity gate refusing the English text with edits. Both stay green while the English section changes underneath them.

That is the one place in this repository where a silent gap reaches a person rather than a number.

What can be mechanised is "the text these five were written against has moved and nobody has looked at them since", which is a question a reviewer can answer. Per-language entries, so a partial catch-up shows as four remaining names instead of being cleared by one edit.

  **Normalised before hashing, and that is what makes the rule keepable.** The section is hard-wrapped prose: a rewrap changes every line and no meaning, and a guard red for that gets switched off long before the amendment it was written for arrives. Quote markers stripped, whitespace collapsed — so a rewrap is invisible and a number inside an otherwise identical sentence is not.

It matches the lead-in, "Sentry" and "sub-processor" instead.

  **Extraction throws rather than returning empty.** A missing or duplicated marker means the English policy was restructured; an empty string would hash to a stable value and report all five current forever.

## The edge that was guarded by a comment (2026-08-22)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Two-list follow-ups" — filed by the run that created the edge an hour earlier: "The reverse import is the one that would cost something and only a comment says so ... bundle-size would catch it late, as a number rather than as a name, and the repo already runs check-analytics-imports for exactly this shape of rule." -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-22] [est: 18000] **The parser stays out of the bundle** — the import direction `lib/i18n-coverage.ts` → `lib/translation-status.ts` relies on, stated as a rule instead of as a comment.

  **The failure it guards is quiet, which is the whole argument for a named case.** The reverse import compiles, every existing suite stays green, and what changes is the size of a file nobody was looking at: `app/settings.tsx` calls `isPartiallyTranslated` per picker row, so `lib/translation-status.ts` ships to the browser, and an import from it to `lib/i18n-coverage.ts` would drag `lib/i18n-source.ts` and `lib/js-tokens.ts` in behind it. `bundle-size` would notice eventually, as a number, on whichever unrelated PR pushed it over.

  **"Imports nothing at all" rather than a list of banned specifiers.** A module with no imports cannot reach the parser by any path, so the rule needs no import graph and cannot be defeated by an intermediate module. It is stricter than the danger on purpose: the question a future failure should provoke is not which specifier is banned but what the settings screen now pays to render a badge, and a deny-list would answer neither.

  **The other half is an allow-list of the modules that DO import the parser, with the reason each never loads.** Two today — `lib/i18n-coverage.ts` and `lib/check-orphan-i18n-keys.ts` — swept out of the whole source tree rather than out of `app/` and `components/`, because the way this breaks is a `lib/` module the app already imports growing the edge. A second case refuses a stale entry: a permission naming a module that no longer imports the parser permits nothing and looks exactly like one that does.

  **The regex gets its own case, because a sweep's green run is "no file matched" either way.** Static `from`, dynamic `import()` and `require()` are all read; a string literal that merely looks like a specifier is not.

  Mutation-checked: adding one import to `lib/translation-status.ts` fails both cases, naming the file and the cost.

## The last 62 keys: a per-locale sweep, not a seventh clever unit (decomposed 2026-08-22)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Home-screen follow-ups" / "Collection-detail-screen follow-ups" / "Screen-family follow-ups" — three consecutive runs have now filed the same endgame: "62 keys remain English in all four ... past the point where a family entry pays for itself. Context, so the next run does not re-derive it:

- **The measurement, taken today.** `be` 87.6% (62 inherited), `pl`/`de`/`es` 86.5% (67 each); `ru` and `en` 100%. 62 base keys are English in ALL four; five more (`myCollections`, `createCollection`, and the three the home screen left) are English in some. 67 keys × 4 locales is the whole remainder.
- **Why not another screen family.** The 62 are spread over ~26 files and the largest holder is `app/friends.tsx` at 9. A family entry costs a table row, a `because` paragraph and a size number to catch nine keys, and the four biggest holders after it hold 8, 8, 7, 7. The unit stopped paying.
- **The crossing is the decision, and it resolves itself if the sweep FINISHES.** Every run since 2026-08-22 morning has filed "when `be` crosses 90, `PARTIALLY_TRANSLATED_LANGUAGES` loses a name while ~60 keys behind it are still English — either 90 is the promise or the threshold wants a second tier". At 100% in all six that argument is empty: nothing is badged because nothing is partial. Finishing is the answer to the question, which is why the sweep is one task rather than a decision followed by a task.
- **What the sweep must not do.** `slavicPlural` for `be`/`pl` and `plural` for `de`/`es` wherever a word follows the count (`__tests__/i18n-agreement.test.ts` enforces the shape); `de` addresses the reader as *du*; each locale's own quotation marks. All three are guarded, so the sweep's mistakes are red rather than shipped.

- [DONE: 2026-08-22] [est: 55000] **1/2 — finish `be` and `pl`** — 62 keys in each, plus whatever either declares partially, taking both to 100%. Both cross the threshold, so both leave `PARTIALLY_TRANSLATED_LANGUAGES` in the same commit that earns it, and the module's note stops predicting the crossing and records it.

  **`pl` was 67 keys behind, not 62, and the five extra are the finding.** `addFriend`, `removeFriend`, `follow`, `unfollow` and `saveCollection` — the social verbs on the profile screen and the button that submits the collection form. `be` translated all five days ago; `pl` never did, and no report named them, because every count this repo publishes is either per-LOCALE (a percentage that averages five keys away) or per-KEY across the four partial locales (which the "English in all four" filter drops the moment one locale translates it). A key three locales have and the fourth does not is the shape that falls between both.

  **The threshold argument three runs filed was answered by not needing an answer.** Every run since the morning of 2026-08-22 wrote down the same dilemma: when `be` crosses 90%, `PARTIALLY_TRANSLATED_LANGUAGES` loses a name while ~60 keys behind it are still English, so either 90 is the promise or the badge wants a second tier. Finishing the locale dissolves it — at 100% there is nothing to qualify and nothing to tier — and that is worth stating as a habit rather than as luck: a threshold argument is only worth having about a locale that stops short of the threshold.

  **The blind spot the sweep itself walked through, closed in the same commit.** Coverage counts DECLARATIONS, so declaring a key and pasting the English text satisfies every percentage in this repository while shipping exactly the string the count was invented to find. `be` at 100% and `be` at 100% with fourteen English strings in it are indistinguishable from every number here. `i18n-complete-families.test.ts` has made that comparison since it was written and only over the keys a LISTED family owns — which covered the work while it was being done family by family, and stopped covering it the moment 129 values were written by hand outside every family. `__tests__/i18n-english-verbatim.test.ts` makes it over the whole map.

  **Its first run is ten exemptions and zero findings, and both halves of that are the point.** Every verbatim value in the tree turned out legitimate: a brand (`templateHotWheelsName`), four loanwords a language genuinely took (`Comics`, `Chat`, `Chats`, `Tags`), `Premium`, an example handle, and two strings with no words in them. So the guard catches nothing today and is written for the next sweep — which is the honest description of a rule written after the work rather than before it.

  **The wordless values are out by a property, not by name.** ``≈ ${amount} ${currency}`` and ``${current} / ${total}`` are identical in six locales because there is one way to write them, and "this locale kept the English text" is not a claim either can be measured against. Gating on letters OUTSIDE the interpolations keeps them out without a line in the exemption list, so the next wordless value added is out for free. Only the gate strips interpolations; the comparison keeps them, because `${slavicPlural(params?.count, "предмет", …)}` is where a paste hides — translated words inside what looks like code.

  **`be` and `pl` floors re-measured from 279/271 to 463.** A floor of 279 under a 498-key map catches nothing a bad merge could do; it was measured when the locale declared 304.

## The base map said "1 items" (2026-08-22)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Plural-agreement follow-ups" — "The same class of defect is wide open in `de` and `es`, and nothing looks for it ... A two-form counterpart to `slavicPlural` plus the same syntactic scan would close it". Filed by the run that fixed the Slavic half, an hour earlier, which named two examples and did not go looking for the rest. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-22] [est: 28000] **Agree the two-form languages too, and put both rules in one module** — 26 values across `en`, `de` and `es`, and the finding is which locale was worst.

  **English. `itemsCount` rendered "1 items", `photosCount` "1 photos", `sharedWithPeople` "Shared with 1 people", `itemsMoved` "Moved 1 items", `galleryOpenPhotos` "Open photo gallery, 1 photos".** Seven keys in the base map — not a translation lagging behind English, but English, in the language every reviewer of this repo reads. The previous run named `de.exportPdfItemCount` and `es.accessOpenFor` as the follow-up and did not scan `en`, because the mental model was "the translations need fixing". The scan was two lines and it answered a question nobody had asked.

  **`de` had six and `es` eight, and two of them need a VERB to agree, not a noun.** `Zugriff haben 1 Personen` and `1 personas tienen acceso` — so the German and Spanish values pass "Person hat"/"Personen haben" and "persona tiene"/"personas tienen" through the same helper. That is why neither function knows any vocabulary: a rule that took a noun and pluralised it could not have expressed either.

  **`lib/plural-slavic.ts` became `lib/plural.ts`, one hour old, and the rename is the point.** Two modules named by language family is how one half gets applied and the other forgotten — which is a description of what had just happened, twice. The question a reader arrives with is "how do plurals work in this map", and it now has one answer with both rules in it.

  **Zero is the interesting number in the two-form rule, and it is why `plural` is written `=== 1` rather than `< 2`.** "0 items", "0 Objekte", "0 objetos" all take the plural, and 0 is what an empty collection shows — a rule phrased as "fewer than two takes the singular" is green at 1 and wrong on the emptiest screen in the app. Pinned in three languages.

  **The guard now covers all six locales and carries five exemptions, each a word that genuinely does not inflect.** *фото* and *фота*, indeclinable loanwords in Russian and Belarusian; `selected` and `remaining` twice, English participles that agree with nothing. Which of the two helpers a locale uses is deliberately NOT checked — that is a question about vocabulary rather than shape, and a syntactic scan claiming to answer it would be claiming more than it can see.

  Sixteen count-bearing keys, six locales: before today four values agreed anything at all; now every one that needs to.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all`, 6378 tests (+4), build.

## "1 предметов" — the defect no coverage number could report (2026-08-22)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Collection-detail-screen follow-ups" — "`be` and `pl` use the invariant genitive plural everywhere and only `ru` has real plural functions ... It is a bug rather than a gap: the keys are DECLARED, so no coverage number reports them". Filed by the previous run, which followed the convention rather than fixing it and said so. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-22] [est: 32000] **Agree the Slavic nouns with their counts, and guard the shape** — one pure module, 19 values across `ru`, `be` and `pl`, and a defect on the most ordinary screen in the app.

  **A collection with one item read "1 предметов", "1 прадметаў", "1 przedmiotów".** The plural under the number one, in three languages, on the first thing a collector sees. `accessOpenFor` read "для 1 человек", `sharedWithPeople` read "Udostępniono 1 osobom", and `pl.photosCount` read "1 zdjęć".

  **Six runs of coverage work could not have found it, and that is the point worth keeping.** Every one of those keys is DECLARED in all six locales, so the coverage percentages count them as done and every family suite passes — they are translated, and translated wrongly. A count of decisions made cannot see a decision made badly. What found it was going looking: sixteen count-bearing keys, printed side by side across four locales, and reading them.

  **The rule was in the tree already, applied to two keys out of sixteen.** `ru.selectedCount` and `ru.deleteItemsTitle` spelled the one/few/many branch out inline, twice, which is how it came to be applied where somebody happened to think of it. `lib/plural-slavic.ts` states it once (renamed to `lib/plural.ts` an hour later, when the two-form languages needed the same treatment); the words stay at the call site, which is what lets `pl` use the same branch for `osoba`/`osoby`/`osób` and different words from `ru`.

  **The scan found a seventeenth key nobody had listed.** `ru.syncingPillA11y` read "1 изменений синхронизируется с облаком" — noun and verb both wrong under one. It is a screen-reader label, so it had no visual reviewer at all.

  **`ru`'s inline rule had a real bug in it, and moving it out fixed that too.** The old `deleteItemsTitle` excluded the teens with `mod100 < 10 || mod100 >= 20`, which also excludes 10 and 15–19 from the "few" branch — harmless, because those end in 0 and 5–9 and never reach it, but it is not the rule. `slavicPlural` uses `mod100 < 12 || mod100 > 14` and `__tests__/plural.test.ts` (then `plural-slavic.test.ts`) pins both sides of the boundary: 11–14 and 111–114 take "many", 21 and 121 come back out.

  **The guard is syntactic and one exemption wide.** In `ru`, `be` and `pl`, a value that interpolates the count and then puts a WORD next to it must call `slavicPlural`. Colon forms, parenthesised counts and values ending on the number agree with nothing and pass untouched. The exemption is *фото* / *фота*, indeclinable in both languages, with a reason per entry and a case that fails if an exemption stops applying. Mutation-checked by restoring `be.itemsCount` to its old flat form, which fails naming the file, the locale and the value.

  **The block-bodied reader shipped this morning lost its last real occupant, so it got a direct case rather than a quiet death.** Both `ru` block bodies became one-line arrows here. `readableText` is now extracted from `valueOf` and exercised on a branching body as a literal — a capability that is correct and unused is the one a future "nothing uses this" removes truthfully and wrongly.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all`, 6374 tests (+16), build.

## The home screen, and a guard that fired on the safe direction (2026-08-22)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Collection-detail-screen follow-ups" — "`app/index.tsx` is still worth one more entry — it is the home screen — and after it the entries are worth less than the sweep". Filed by the previous run in the same breath as the measurement that named it. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-22] [est: 30000] **Translate the home screen, and correct yesterday's margin guard to the one direction churn can move** — 19 keys across be, pl, de and es, and a case fixed by the failure it produced an hour after being written.

  **The most MIXED screen listed so far, which is the case the screen family exists for.** `createCollection` was declared in five locales and missing only in `de`; `myCollections` was missing in three; `friends`, `following`, `statsTitle` and `recentlyAdded` in all four. So a German reader met "Meine Sammlungen" above two English tiles and an English "Statistics" heading — a screen half-translated key by key rather than section by section, which no per-key gap count surfaces and which the family closes in one demand. 25 keys after 12 prefix-owned and `appName` are subtracted; 19 of them needed work.

  **The margin case shipped this morning fired on `be` at 87.6%, and the guard was wrong, not the number.** It compared `Math.abs(coverage − 90) < 4`, which treats both sides of the threshold as risky. They are not: denominator drift is one-directional. Adding English keys lowers every row that has not translated them and raises nobody, so a locale BELOW the threshold cannot cross it by churn — `be` can only reach 90 by somebody translating twelve more keys, and the parity case goes red on the run that does it. The reclassification churn CAN cause is the other one, a complete locale falling under and getting badged by a PR nobody thought was about translations, and `ru`/`en` at 100% are ten points clear of that.

  Widening the margin was the available wrong repair, and it is the one a symmetric guard teaches: the case now checks only locales at or above the threshold, and says in its own comment why the version it replaces was wrong.

  **`be` is now twelve keys from 90%, so the badge decision lands on the next screen family.** When `be` crosses, `PARTIALLY_TRANSLATED_LANGUAGES` loses a name and the picker stops qualifying Belarusian while roughly 60 keys behind it are still English. That arrives as a red parity case rather than as a silent reclassification, which is the right place to have the argument — recorded in `lib/translation-status.ts` so whoever meets the red suite meets the reasoning with it.

  Mutation-checked: raising the margin to 12 names `ru` and `en` and no partial locale, which is the asymmetry working.

  Coverage: be 84.9% → 87.6%, pl 83.5% → 86.5%, de 82.7% → 86.5%, es 82.9% → 86.5%. Keys English in all four partial locales: 75 → 62.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all`, 6358 tests (+5), build.

The suggestion's own advice — re-run the measurement, do not trust the entry — was right in the direction it did not expect.

  **The verbatim check could not read `ru`'s plural keys, and the family could not be added until it could.** `valueOf` knew two shapes, a string literal and a one-expression arrow. `ru` declares `selectedCount` and `deleteItemsTitle` as arrows with a BLOCK body, because Russian needs three plural forms and a ternary cannot spell them — so the reader returned `null`, and `null` is what the verbatim case reads as "nothing to compare". The two keys most likely to be copied wholesale, where the English sits inside what looks like code, were the two it silently skipped. Rewritten over `LocaleBlock.values`, the brace-aware value span the parser already isolates, which handles all three shapes and deletes two regexes.

Only `ru` has real plural functions. Following the convention rather than fixing it here was deliberate: four locales' worth of plural rules is its own task, and doing it inside a translation run would have buried it. Filed.

The case was written when the four partials sat near 40% and nothing came near the boundary — so a between-groups gap measures how little has been translated, and could only be restored by stopping. Replaced with the thing the gap was a proxy for, per row: no locale may sit within 4 points of the 90% threshold, which is two feature PRs' worth of denominator drift at ~5 keys to the point. It is a tripwire and is meant to fire — `be` is 5.1 points clear, about one more screen family — and the answer then is to decide what the Belarusian badge says at 90%, not to widen the margin.

## A percentage of something nobody can finish (2026-08-22)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Per-screen i18n follow-ups" — "`appName` and `emailPlaceholder` still subtract from every percentage ... an `UNTRANSLATABLE_KEYS` set excluded from the denominator would make `ru` read 100% and make the four partial rows a percentage of what is actually translatable. - [DONE: 2026-08-22] [est: 14000] **Take the brand name and the email address out of the denominator** — `appName` ("Collectables") and `emailPlaceholder` (`you@example.com`) are correct in English in all six locales, and every coverage row counted them as gaps. `ru` read 99.6% and could not be moved: the missing 0.4% was a brand and an address, under a threshold somebody would eventually be tempted to lower rather than a number anybody could raise.

  **The list is the one exemption this module has, so it is built to be hard to grow.** `UNTRANSLATABLE_KEYS` is a Record of key → REASON, not a set: an exemption is a key nobody has to justify again, and the cheapest defence is making the justification something a reader meets. The membership test is written down — would a translator handed this key correctly hand it back unchanged — and a case caps the list at five while another refuses a reason shorter than twenty characters.

  **`translatable` is a new field rather than a redefinition of `baseKeys`.** One number would silently mean the second while being read as the first; `501 keys in the map, 499 of them translatable` is the honest pair, and `formatCoverageRow` now names the exemptions on any row that uses one so a reader who sees a denominator of 499 does not go looking for the difference.

  **The staleness check could not live in the module, and finding that out was the useful part.** Shipped first as a throw inside `translationCoverage` — an exemption naming a key the base map does not declare removes nothing and looks exactly like one that works — and it turned three cases red immediately: that function is handed ANY source, and every hand-checkable fixture in the suite declares four keys, none of them a brand name. So the arithmetic stays general and inert, and the claim about THIS repository's map is asserted in the suite against the real map, where it can be made honestly. Mutation-checked by planting a `goneKey` exemption: two cases fail, and the second one's message was rewritten because it said "every locale declares it" for a key the base map does not have — right finding, wrong sentence.

  **The old case that pinned `ru`'s two inherited keys by name survives as two.** `inherited` is a WORKLIST now and `ru`'s is empty; the exemptions it is using are reported by `untranslatable`, so a third untranslated key arriving in `ru` still fails one case and a third exemption fails another. A locale that declares an exempt key anyway is credited with it rather than reported as having a gap.

## The reverse index the orphan guard was throwing away (2026-08-22)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Per-screen i18n follow-ups" — "A per-screen family needs a way to know what a screen renders, and the repo already has most of it ... extracting that as a reusable reader is the enabling step, and it is smaller than the family rule that would sit on top of it". Taken as filed: the entry above it says the prefix unit is spent at 175 keys, and the per-screen unit cannot be built until something can say what a screen reads. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-22] [est: 18000] **Extract `lib/i18n-key-usage.ts` — file → keys, key → files — and make the orphan guard a projection of it** — `check-orphan-i18n-keys` walked every source file, collected the key-position literals of each, and poured them into one flat `mentioned` set. The file each literal came from was gone by the time the answer was formed, so the walk computed the per-screen fact and discarded it to answer the smaller question.

  **The extraction is the enabling half and stops there, deliberately.** It indexes; it does not judge. No coverage rule, no screen table, no threshold — those are the thing that sits on top, and filing them into the same module is how an index becomes a rule nobody can reuse. What moved is `collectStringLiterals`, its regex and the whole "what counts as a READ" argument (four forms, five key positions, the `?? "you"` that made position matter); what stayed in `check-orphan-i18n-keys.ts` is the orphan QUESTION — which keys came back with no readers, which locales declare them, and how that reads as a report. That file is 143 lines shorter and now says what it is about.

  **Both maps are complete over what was asked, and that is the design decision worth the empty arrays.** Every scanned file has an entry in `byFile` and every key handed in has one in `byKey`, empty where there is nothing to report. A sparse map cannot tell "this screen renders no translated text" from "this screen was not scanned" — and a per-screen rule that could not tell them apart would read an unwalked directory as a screen with nothing left to translate, which is the failure mode that makes a green guard worse than none. Mutation-checked by making `byFile` sparse: two cases fail, naming both halves.

  **`findOrphanI18nKeys` throws where the index would shrug.** No base map means no keys asked about, which the index answers honestly with "nothing unread" — a green run and not a finding. So the base-map lookup keeps its throw, now in a `baseKeys()` of its own that says why in three lines rather than by sitting inline.

  **Per-file lists come back in BASE-MAP order**, not in the order literals appear in the file: keys belonging to one feature are adjacent in the map, so a family reads as a run. Per-key lists keep scan order, which is the caller's own — the walk sorts, so the list reads alphabetically without this module claiming to have sorted anything.

  **The new suite runs over the real tree, which is the case the extraction is FOR.** An index that credited `app/` with nothing would pass every unit case and be useless to build a rule on; the suite asserts more than half the screens read a key, that every key it names is a base key, and that the index and the guard agree on there being no orphans. Floors rather than counts, since the exact numbers are a chore and the claim is about the index being populated.

  **The seven suites that ask this question by hand are the follow-up, not this task.** `share-sheet`, `bulk-bar-extraction`, `edit-collection-modal`, `move-collection-modal`, `collection-share-sheet`, `photo-lightbox-item-card` and `a11y-icon-labels` each match `t\("key"` against one file's text — the per-screen question, answered with the rule this repo already measured as wrong for 43 of 502 keys. Nothing is red today because those screens all use the direct call; the first one to store a key in a table would go red while still rendering it. Filed with the counts.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6322 tests (+14), build.

## Six families said du, five strings said Sie, and nobody had written it down (2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Per-screen i18n follow-ups" — "German has now followed *du* for six consecutive families and the app-wide question is still open ... the sweep is now smaller than the argument". - [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 12000] **Settle the German register, and make the decision a rule rather than a comment** — the empty states, wishlist, filters, search, marketplace and collection families all address the reader as *du*; `templatePickerHint`, `homeSubtitle`, `languageSubtitle`, `deleteAccountText` and `deleteAccountFailed` still said *Sie*. Six local calls in one direction is not a split, it is an unrecorded decision with five exceptions, and a German speaker reads the mix as two people having written the app.

  **The direction was chosen by precedent, not preference**, and the entry says so: converting five strings is a smaller change than converting six families back, and reverting is now the larger edit — which is the point of writing it as an enforceable rule instead of a note somebody has to find.

  **The suggestion said three strings; it is five.** Corrected on contact, the third time today a promoted entry's count was wrong in the direction of "smaller than it is". Worth noticing as a pattern in how these get filed rather than as three separate slips.

  **The string that shaped the rule is the one NOT changed.** `marketplaceListingNotFoundHint` reads `Sie wurde möglicherweise vom Eigentümer entfernt` — third-person feminine agreeing with *die Anzeige*, rendering "It may have been removed by its owner". A sweep flagging every capitalised `Sie` would have "fixed" that into nonsense. So the guard matches two constructions that are unambiguously formal ADDRESS — capitalised `Ihr…`/`Ihnen`, and `<verb>en Sie` — and leaves the pronoun alone. A case names that string explicitly, because it is what explains the rule's shape.

  **The mutation check caught a defect in the guard's own message.** Reverting one string to `Wählen Sie` failed correctly but quoted `hlen Sie`: JS `\w` is ASCII-only, so the match started mid-word at the umlaut. It caught the defect and misdescribed it — which is the half of a guard that decides whether the next reader trusts it. The class is now `[A-Za-zÄÖÜäöüß]`, and the message names `Wählen Sie` in full.

  A vacuity case asserts `de` says *du* at least twenty times, since every other assertion here is satisfied by a locale that addresses nobody at all.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6308 tests (+3), build.

## Two lists of the same roots, and a test to catch them drifting (2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Scan-root follow-ups" — "`FLOOR_WALKS` and `count.roots` are two tables naming the same roots, kept in step by one test". The entry offered two shapes and called the bigger one cleaner; it is, and this is it. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 22000] **Derive the walk list from the floors instead of listing it twice** — `FLOOR_WALKS` named the roots of each multi-root walk for the re-measure tool, `count.roots` named the same roots for the guard, and a test asserted they agreed. A list, a second list, and a check that they matched, for one fact.

  **The floor entry wins the tie because it is the one the GUARD asserts against.** A table that disagreed with it would document a property the binary does not enforce, which is the failure this module exists to refuse, one level up. `extensions` moved into `ScannedCountFloor` beside `roots` — a walk is completely described by those two — and `floorWalks()` is now a projection over `SCANNED_FLOORS`. A guard joins the list by declaring `count.roots`, which is also what makes it a multi-root walk, so the two can no longer drift: nothing to add when a guard adopts roots, nothing to remove when one stops.

  **`checkWalkPremise` took a whole `FloorWalk` and only ever read its extensions.** It takes the extension list now, which is what the pure cases were always really about — three of them were constructing a walk object to pass one field.

  **The vacuity guard had to be rewritten, and got stronger for it.** It asked `floorWalks()` which of its entries lacked roots, which is unanswerable-by-construction once that list IS the entries with roots — a case that cannot fail is the thing it was written to prevent. It now asks the WRAPPERS: a guard whose `SCANNED_DIRS` names more than one directory must declare `count.roots`. That catches the new multi-root guard that declares nothing — which the hand-kept table would simply have omitted, silently, since being absent from `FLOOR_WALKS` was indistinguishable from not being a walk. Mutation-checked by dropping `roots` from `check-inline-hex`: it fails naming the guard.

  The parity test is gone, replaced by one that pins the derivation — the walk's roots must be the SAME reference as the floor's, so a future edit that copies rather than projects is caught.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6293 tests, build. Run immediately before the commit this time, after the mutation check rather than before it.

A threshold that failed would have reintroduced the chore the whole change existed to remove.

The two halves stay exported for the single-root guards that only want the count, and the function REFUSES an entry that declares no roots rather than falling back, so "which premises did this guard assert?" stays answerable from the call site.

## Multi-root floors: state the property instead of approximating it with a number (decomposed 2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Action-row follow-ups" / "Relationship-table follow-ups" / "Quotation-guard follow-ups" — three consecutive runs have now filed "a multi-root floor expressed as a COUNT has to be re-measured every time the repo grows". Decomposed rather than taken whole: what looked like "divide by the walk size" turns out to be a design change to how six guards state their premise, and the obvious version of it is circular. -->

Context, so the next run does not re-derive it:

- **The chore.** `__tests__/lint-guard-partial-root.test.ts` enforces `minimum > largestRoot` for the six multi-root floors. It goes red whenever an ordinary file lands in the largest root — five times now, twice this afternoon from ONE new component — and the failure surfaces in a suite unrelated to the change that caused it.
- **The obvious fix is circular and must not be attempted.** A floor cannot be `fraction × walkSize` evaluated at guard time: the walk size IS the count being checked, so `count >= 0.75 × count` is always true. The fraction only means something to a tool that already knows what the tree SHOULD hold, which is the committed number again.
- **`remeasure-floors --write` is ruled out by design, not by effort.** `scripts/remeasure-floors.ts` says so in its own header: "a script that rewrote the table would be a script that erased the reasoning the table exists to hold." Do not add it.
- **The property already exists, one layer away.** `checkWalkPremise`'s `empty_root` code says "this root walked to zero files" — the direct, number-free form of "no scan root silently vanished". It is computed by `remeasure-floors` and by the test suite, and NOT by the guards at runtime, which is the only reason the total-count floor has to carry the property by arithmetic.

So the work is: give the guards the per-root property at runtime, then retire the arithmetic that was standing in for it.

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 45000] **1/3 — teach `SCANNED_FLOORS` a `roots` list and prove it on `check-inline-radius`** — add `ScannedCountFloor.roots?`, a `no_root_files` failure code, a pure `evaluateScannedRoots(files, roots)` and an `assertScannedRoots(checkName, files)` beside the existing count assertion; validate `roots` the way `inputs` is already validated (blank entry, duplicate). Adopt it in one guard, end to end, so the design is proved by a wrapper rather than by a unit test. The count floor stays exactly as it is — this slice adds a premise, it removes nothing.

  **Shipped, and the payoff showed up inside the slice.** `ScannedCountFloor.roots?`, two failure codes (`no_root_files` about the tree, `no_roots_floor` about a wrapper/entry mismatch), three entry problems validated the way `inputs` already is (`empty_roots`, `blank_root`, `duplicate_root`), a pure `evaluateScannedRoots` and an `assertScannedRoots`. `check-inline-radius` calls it, and a tree holding only `components/` — 46 files, the exact count that drew level with the floor of 46 twice this afternoon — now refuses with "scan root app/ contributed 0 of the 46 source file(s) this walk found" instead of "below the committed floor, re-measure". No number in it.

  **Roots are asserted BEFORE the count, deliberately.** When a root vanishes both fire and they give opposite advice: `no_root_files` names the directory that went quiet, `below_floor` says "if the tree legitimately shrank, re-measure the floor" — the wrong fix, and the easy one. The specific diagnosis should be the one the reader meets. That reorder is why `check-inline-radius`'s partial fixture changed from "all of app/" to "three files from each root": the dropped-root case moved to its own block, so both codes keep a real wrapper run instead of one shadowing the other.

  **`floor-walks.test.ts`'s largest-root case now skips entries that declare roots**, which is the whole point — that case is the one that has gone red five times on ordinary growth. `check-inline-radius` is out of it. A new parity case pins that a floor's declared roots equal its `FLOOR_WALKS` roots, since two tables now describe one walk and only one of them refuses.

  Every exhaustive record in the harness earned its keep: eleven compile errors across four suites, each naming a code with no fixture, no sentence or no wrapper run. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6280 tests (+43), build.

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 25000] **2/3 — roll `roots` out to the other five multi-root guards** — `check-inline-hex`, `check-analytics-imports`, `check-clarity-input-mask`, `check-a11y-jsx`, `check-problem-phrasing-imports`. Mechanical once 1/3 lands: one field per entry, one call per wrapper. The partial-root fixture harness gains a red run per guard.

  **Shipped. All six now refuse a dropped root by name** — verified by spawning each guard against a tree holding only its first root: every one exits 1 with "scan root components/ contributed 0 of the 19 source file(s) this walk found" rather than a floor number.

  **The fixtures generalised instead of being copied five times.** Each guard's `PARTIAL_FIXTURES` spec used to hand over one whole root (`["app"]`), which was the shortest route to a walk that came up short — and stopped being that once the per-root assertion started running first, since a tree missing `components/` now refuses by NAME. `sliceOfEveryRoot(checkName)` reads `FLOOR_WALKS` for the roots AND the extensions (a markup-only walk handed `.ts` files counts zero of them and reproduces `no_files`, the code the empty-root harness already owns) and takes three files from each. `check-problem-phrasing-imports` went from all of `app/` to fifteen files; that does NOT make it affordable in the dropped-root block, which still copies a whole root per fixture.

  **Two things the migration broke, both caught by the harness rather than by review.** The `no_roots_floor` fixture pointed at `check-inline-hex`, which now declares roots — so it produced `no_root_files` and the exhaustive producer map failed on the mismatch. Repointed at `check-env-inlining`, a single-root walk where naming roots would be the floor and the root saying the same thing twice, with a comment saying why it cannot be one of the six.

  **The arithmetic case in `floor-walks.test.ts` now covers nothing, and says so.** With all six skipped it is a loop that passes exactly like a loop that checked everything — the failure mode this whole module exists to refuse. A new case publishes the count of walks still holding the property by a committed number (zero) and names them when it is not, so the migration finishing is visible rather than inferred. The case itself is kept as the safety net for the next multi-root guard, which will arrive without roots and be caught on its first red run.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6281 tests, build.

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 30000] **3/3 — retire `minimum > largestRoot` for the six, and say what the count floors are still for** — with the property enforced directly, the arithmetic is a second statement of it and the only thing that ever needs re-measuring. The count floors should NOT be deleted: they still catch a walk that returns implausibly few files (a glob that broke without emptying a root), which is a different failure. This slice is mostly the ARGUMENT — which suite keeps which claim, what `remeasure-floors` prints once `MOVE` is no longer a thing that can happen, and whether `FLOOR_SLACK` survives. Do not start it before 2/3 is green.

  **Shipped, and the argument came out as predicted with one correction.** The count floors are NOT deleted and `FLOOR_SLACK` survives. What changed is which question the number answers: the no-single-root property belongs to `roots`, and the count now owns only the other quiet failure — a walk that came back implausibly SMALL with every root present (a narrowed glob, an extension filter that stopped matching, a widened skip list). No per-root check can see that, and none should; it is a question about the size of the result, which is what a floor is.

  **`holds` and `actionable` are two fields on purpose.** `holds` stays the arithmetic FACT, computed identically for every row, so a `declared_roots` row can report `false` and be in perfect health. `actionable` is the policy and the only thing the tool exits 1 for. Collapsing them would have meant a number that means different things per row, which is the ambiguity this module refuses everywhere else.

  **The suggestion drops `largestRoot + 1` once roots are declared, and that is the point rather than a side effect.** That term pushed floors UP as the tree grew, and a floor pushed up trips on ordinary shrinkage. Under the slack reading alone the six numbers barely move — 174, 48, 48, 48, 48, 535 — because the notes were already doing ~25% arithmetic and the largest-root term mostly did not bind. What it did do was TRIGGER re-measures. Nothing now goes red on ordinary growth.

  **Two places tell the reader the durable fix before the recurring one.** `formatFloorMeasurement` and the arithmetic case's failure message both say "declare this guard's roots" before "re-measure", and a case asserts that ORDER — re-measuring is the fix that has to be done again next quarter, and a tool that names it first schedules a chore instead of ending one. A `declared_roots` row sitting under its largest root also says so out loud rather than printing a silent `ok`: a reader who remembers the old rule would otherwise read it as the tool having stopped checking.

  **All six notes rewritten** to say what their number is for now, plus the module header, which had justified the floor with the `app/`-lost-but-`lib/`-kept example that is now the roots' job.

  Mutation-checked by putting `check-inline-radius` back on arithmetic and dropping its floor under `components/`: `MOVE` returns, offers declaring roots first, and both the arithmetic case and the vacuity-count case go red. The safety net for the next multi-root guard is real.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6286 tests, build.

## The third copy, and the tab that was answering its own question (2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Action-row follow-ups" — "`app/friends.tsx` is a THIRD copy of these styles and nobody had filed it", written an hour earlier by the run that extracted the component and found the third copy while scanning for related defects. Taken immediately because the entry names the behaviour-preserving version explicitly, and because a third copy left in place is how the first two came back. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 24000] **Give the friends screen the third surface it had been improvising** — `app/friends.tsx` rendered the same pills from a three-way ternary on the TAB (`"request" | "friend" | "following"`) with its own copy of `actions`, `primaryAction`, `primaryActionText`, `secondaryAction` and `secondaryActionText`. Not a third instance of the table — it branches on a tab, not a relationship — which is exactly why it had survived two extractions.

  **Every difference it had was defensible, and none of it was written down.** The friends tab offers chat and unfriend but no unfollow; the following tab offers unfollow but no add-friend. Both omissions are the tab declining to restate its own heading — a friend listed under "Friends" is not there to be unfollowed, and the following list is a tidying screen, not a prompt to befriend everyone in it. Those are good decisions that existed only as the absence of JSX. `ProfileSurface` gained `"tab"` and they are entries, with `EVERYWHERE` / `BROWSING` / `DETAIL_ONLY` / `DETAIL_AND_TAB` named for what they mean rather than for their members. Not one button changed, and a case asserts that by reading the three deleted lists back out of the table.

  **`getRelationship` would have compiled, passed, and changed what the following tab shows.** It answers `friend` before it answers `following`, so a friend the user also follows renders as a friend — while the tab lists everyone in `following` regardless. The screen therefore maps `kind` through an explicit `RELATIONSHIP_BY_TAB` record: the tab is the question being asked, so the tab decides. A case pins that `getRelationship` is not called here, comments stripped — the record's own doc block names it to explain why it is absent, and the previous run learnt this same lesson twice in one afternoon.

  **`SURFACE_RELATIONSHIPS` is the new half worth keeping.** A surface's shorter button list is only defensible if it is shorter because the surface serves fewer RELATIONSHIPS, and until now nothing said which ones a surface sees. `tab` sees three; `detail` and `row` see five. It is checked in both directions — a relationship a surface names must render something, and one it does not name must render nothing — which is what stops `tab` quietly growing a `request_sent` badge the friends screen has no list to put it in. It also fixed a case written an hour earlier: "has something to render on every surface except self" was true for two surfaces and became false the moment a third arrived, which is the kind of assertion that gets weakened rather than re-derived. Mutation-checked both ways: giving the following tab an add-friend button fails two cases naming the button and the tab, and giving `tab` a `request_sent` badge fails with "tab renders request_sent without naming it".

  **The friends screen's dispatcher throws on `follow`, for the same reason the people list throws on `chat`.** Every list on that screen is of people the user already has an edge to, so `follow` is unreachable — and a table edit that made it reachable should surface as a throw rather than as a button that does nothing.

  Three registries let `app/friends.tsx` go: it leaves `radius-pill-adoption` (its only `RADIUS_PILL` consumers were these buttons), drops to `list: false` in `spacing-gap-adoption`, and gives up four tokens in `design-tokens`. All three follow the precedent `<HeroBanner>` set — the note says where the token went, not that it vanished. No floors moved: no new file.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6237 tests, build. No DB migration, no creds, no new i18n keys.
