## The decision table written out twice, and the one place it disagreed with itself (2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Pressable-role follow-ups" — "`app/people.tsx` and `app/profile/[id].tsx` both write the relationship-action buttons once per branch" and "`app/profile/[id].tsx` writes the same four buttons five times", filed 2026-08-20 by two different runs. Picked from screen code after four consecutive i18n tasks. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 21000] **Extract `lib/relationship-actions.ts` so the profile buttons are a table rather than ten ternary branches** — `app/profile/[id].tsx` had five relationship branches holding sixteen `<Pressable>`s and `app/people.tsx` five more holding ten, every one of them the same four lines differing only in label key and handler. A decision table with six inputs, written twice, comparable only by reading both.

  **They already disagreed, and nothing recorded whether that was deliberate.** A friend's DETAIL screen offers chat, unfriend and unfollow; a friend's ROW in the people list offers unfriend alone. That is a good decision — a list row is not the place for three buttons — but until now the only evidence for it was two blocks of JSX, and both readings were available to whoever looked next: "the row is missing two buttons" or "the detail screen has two extra". It is now `surfaces: ["detail"]` on two entries, and a case asserts `friend` is the ONLY relationship where the two surfaces differ, so a second divergence has to be chosen on purpose.

  **Intents, not function names.** `reject_request`, `cancel_request` and `remove_friend` are three ids mapping to one `removeFriend` call, because the social graph has one edge-removal operation and three things a person can mean by it. That is what lets the labels differ ("Reject" / "Cancel invitation" / "Remove friend") without three call sites deciding independently which function that implies — and it is asserted, because it is exactly the duplication somebody would "notice" and collapse. Each screen switches on `ACTION_METHOD[id]` rather than on the intent, so the mapping lives in the table; the people list's dispatcher THROWS on `chat` rather than falling through, since a silent no-op is how a table edit that gave a row a chat button would go unnoticed.

  `TranslationKey` is now exported from `lib/i18n-context.tsx` so the table can name a key it does not render. The import is type-only and therefore erased — that module pulls react-native and cannot be loaded by `tsx --test` — and it is what makes a typo'd label key fail in the table rather than at every call site. Sixteen `<Pressable>`s became nine and ten became four.

  **Two scanned floors had to be re-measured, and the pattern is now the finding.** One new `lib/` file drew `check-inline-hex`'s floor level with `lib/`'s own count (168), and one new test file drew `check-problem-phrasing-imports`'s level with `__tests__/`'s (450). Both were raised with `npm run remeasure-floors` (174 and 535) and both notes now say what the fourth re-measurement for the same reason means: these floors track repository GROWTH rather than guard coverage, and a floor expressed as a fraction of the walk would state the property directly instead of restating a number. That guard behaved exactly as designed — it noticed the moment its own number stopped proving anything — which is why the observation belongs in the note rather than in a complaint.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6220 tests (+17), build. No DB migration, no creds, no new i18n keys.

## The quote that was opened in Polish and closed in English (2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Filter-family follow-ups" — "Nothing checks that a locale's quotation marks are its own" and "`search*` (14) is the next family". One task, because the guard is what protects the quoting decisions the previous run made by hand, and writing it found six defects nobody had filed. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 20000] **Translate the search family, and check that each locale quotes with its own marks** — the search overlay was the awkward half-translated case: four of its eighteen keys (`searchClose`, both in-collection strings, the overlay a11y label) were declared in every locale and the other fourteen in none, so the SAME overlay showed a translated placeholder above English result headings. 14 keys × 4 locales closes it.

  **The guard found six real defects on its first run, none of them in a family this task touched.** Two `chatsEmptyHint` strings used Russian-style guillemets in Polish and German — `kliknij «Wiadomość»`, `tippe auf «Nachricht»` — which is what happens when somebody translates from the `ru` string and carries its punctuation across. And four marketplace message templates opened with the correct locale mark and closed with an **ASCII `"`**: `` `…ogłoszenie „${title}"…` ``. Inside a template literal that is perfectly legal code, which is exactly why it survived — the compiler has no opinion, and the user sees `„Tytuł"`. All six fixed.

  **The rule cannot be "no English quotes", and that is the whole design.** `de` closes with `“` and `pl` closes with `”`, so both English marks are legitimate somewhere in this file. What is checkable is narrower and exact: every curly mark inside a locale's block must be one of THAT locale's two. It still catches the paste — an English `“…”` in `de` brings a `”` that `de` never uses, and in `pl` brings a `“` that `pl` never uses. A second case counts openers against closers per locale, which is what catches the half-converted string where both marks happen to be legal for that locale; it is the case that found the four ASCII closers.

  **The suite reported its own explanatory comment first.** The header inserted above each locale's filter block named all four pairs — `«…» for be, „…” for pl` — so every mark appeared in every block and four locales failed on prose about themselves. Fixed by blanking comments through `stripComments` before scanning, and the comment now points at the suite instead of repeating it. Worth recording because a rule that fires on documentation of itself is a rule that gets exempted rather than fixed.

  **Fourth family, and `be` is now at 60.7%** (pl 58.9%, es 58.3%, de 58.1%) — from 46.4%/44.6%/44.0%/43.8% this morning. Floors re-measured at the usual ~8% slack (be 279, pl 271, de 267, es 268). Mutation-checked both new rules: an English `“` planted in `pl` fails naming the mark and the locale's own pair, and a `de` string with an ASCII closer fails with 12 openers against 11 closers.

## The error message you read while already stuck (2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Complete-family follow-ups" — "`filter*` is the next family and it is the one that will test the harness", written by the run before this one, which named both hazards in advance: a function-valued key and four strings built around typographic quotes. Both turned out to be real, and one of them was a hole in the harness rather than in the translation. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 19000] **Translate the item-filter family, and teach the family rule to read function-valued keys** — 19 of the 20 `filter*` keys were English in all four partial locales (`filterClearSearch` was the lone exception). The filter sheet opens from every list screen, and **eight of the twenty strings are validation errors** — the messages a user reads while already stuck, which is the worst possible moment to switch languages on them.

  **The typographic quotes were the predicted hazard and they were handled per locale, not pasted.** English writes `“Price from” must be a number.`; `ru` already used `«…»`. The four new locales use their own: `«…»` for `be`, `„…”` for `pl`, `„…“` for `de`, `«…»` for `es`. Pasting the English pair would have been invisible to every check in this repo and wrong on four locales' terms.

  **The function-valued key was the predicted hazard that turned out to be a hole in the HARNESS.** `filterActive` is `(params) => \`Filters (${params?.count ?? 0})\`` — the first function value to land in a listed family. `valueOf` read string literals only and returned `null` for it, so the verbatim-English check silently skipped it: a locale could declare `` `Filters (${…})` `` with the English word sitting inside what looks like code and satisfy every count in the file. The suite would have gone green on exactly the mistake it exists to catch, on the one key most likely to be copied wholesale.

  So the reader now also takes a function's template body. The interpolation is deliberately left in rather than stripped — it is identical across locales, so it neither hides a difference nor invents one, and stripping it correctly would need a second brace-aware walk. **And a new case asserts that the function keys ARE being compared**, because a reader that quietly went back to returning `null` would make the verbatim check skip them and stay green — the failure this whole paragraph is about, one level up. Mutation-checked both ways: an English `filterActive` in `pl` fails naming the key, and sabotaging the template reader fails with "is function-valued and is not being compared".

  **Third family, and the coverage numbers keep moving for the right reason.** be 57.9%, pl 56.1%, es 55.5%, de 55.3% — all four gained 19 more. Floors re-measured at the usual ~8% slack (be 266, pl 258, de 254, es 255). The `publishes the honest number` case reads 0 below half for the second run running; its caveat about the count being published rather than ratcheted is unchanged and still the important part.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6183 tests (+7), build. No DB migration, no creds, no new base keys.

So the family is pinned all-or-nothing — a count would let the next person add a 25th English key and leave it forever. Two failure modes, not one: a missing declaration, and a declaration carrying the English text verbatim, which satisfies the coverage counter exactly as well as a real translation does. ## The keys were checked and the values were not (2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Clamp-and-control follow-ups" — "`premium_upsell_shown` now has three props and nothing constrains their VALUES", written an hour earlier by the run that added the third prop. Picked deliberately from analytics rather than the visibility row a third time. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 21000] **Give the closed-set analytics props a table of allowed values, and enforce it on the same policy the key check uses** — `assertValidProps` has always validated the payload's KEYS against `ANALYTICS_EVENTS[name].props`: dev throws, production warns and strips. The values under those keys were free strings, and the value is the more expensive of the two to get wrong. An unknown key makes a column the schema doc has never heard of and nobody has built a report on. `source: "collectionEdit"` makes a second bucket inside a column somebody HAS, splitting a funnel that is then read as a number that looks too low — the failure mode where nothing looks broken and the conclusion is wrong.

  **`lib/analytics-prop-values.ts` holds eleven sets, and none of them is a hand-written list.** Each is built by `closedSet<T>(members: Record<T, true>)`, which is exhaustive in BOTH directions: a member the union gained and the table did not is a missing property, and a member the table has and the union does not is an excess property. A plain `readonly T[]` would happily accept a table listing two of a union's five members, which is exactly the drift the module exists to prevent. `MarketplaceMode`, `CollectionVisibility`, `CurrencyValueError`, `AnalyticsRelationship`, `PremiumIntentSource`, `PremiumUpsellControl` and `AppLanguage` all bind this way — the last through a type-only import, because `lib/i18n-context.tsx` pulls react-native and cannot be LOADED by a node CLI, while its types cost nothing at runtime.

  **What is deliberately NOT constrained is the load-bearing half.** Ids (`collectionId`, `targetUserId`), counts (`photoCount`) and anything from outside the app (`provider`, whatever Supabase reports) have no set: a table that has to be exempted is a table that stops being read. Booleans have none for a different reason — `AnalyticsProps` already types them, and `[true, false]` would restate the compiler. A vacuity case guards the whole feature, because every other case here passes trivially if `values` is absent everywhere, which is what a bad merge leaves behind.

  **`source` is per-event, not per-prop-name, and that is the design.** `premium_activated.source` may be `server_sync` (the cloud restored an entitlement); `premium_upsell_shown.source` may not, because nobody SAW a gate. One global table keyed by prop name would have to allow it for both, so the sets live inside each event definition and a case states the difference by exercising both.

  **The production policy is deliberately asymmetric with the key check, and the reason is stated as a case.** An unknown key is dropped — nothing was built on it. An out-of-set VALUE is KEPT, because it sits under a key a report already reads and dropping it would delete what actually happened on the grounds that the taxonomy is out of date, leaving a breakdown whose buckets no longer add up to the event count. That is a harder thing to notice than one extra bar. Dev throws either way, so a kept value only ever means one that reached production without anybody running the dev build.

  **Three things the tables caught on the way in, none of which was a typo.** `signup_completed.method` has a legitimate THIRD value: `simulateSignupEvent()` fires `method: "manual_test"` to check the PostHog wire end-to-end, and it runs in dev — the runtime where an undeclared value throws. Declared, with the reason, rather than exempting the call site and leaving a `method` breakdown unexplained. `__tests__/analytics-validate.test.ts` had used `mode: "sale"` as its fixture in nine places — the wrong spelling of `"sell"`, in the suite that exists to check payloads. And `listing_created`'s description told the report builder `mode` = sale/trade/swap; two of those three values have never existed. The rendered set beside each key is now generated from the type instead of remembered, which is the durable half of that fix — `docs/powerbi-connection.md` shows `` `mode` (`sell` | `trade`) `` and the sets are sorted so reordering a union does not rewrite the doc.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6167 tests (+22), build. No DB migration, no creds, no i18n keys. Verified no live call site sends an out-of-set value — every one of them passes a typed union through (`listing.mode`, `relationshipForAnalytics(...)`, `useI18n().language`), which is why the dev throw ships without breaking a screen.

"private") !== "private"` for itself. Same three clauses, a fourth screen away from the chip they have to agree with, and free to drift in exactly the way the two rows already had.

That makes it defence in depth rather than dead code, and it is kept — the day the chip is bypassed it is the only thing between that bypass and a private collection on a free account. What it is not is a place to restate the rule, so it is now `clampVisibilityForSave(isPremium, editVisibility, collection.visibility ?? "private")` in `lib/premium-helpers.ts`, one branch over `isPrivateVisibilityLocked`. Being unreachable at runtime is precisely why it needed pinning: nothing exercises it, so a regression to a fourth hand-written spelling would look like working code forever. That matters because the standing `[needs-data]` decision further down this file is specifically about the CHIP: with the two merged, taps on the SENTENCE would keep it alive forever, and hiding it would look like it cost traffic it never had. `control: "chip" | "hint"` is now a required argument of each screen's `showPrivateUpsell`, so a new call site cannot forget it, and it is registered in `ANALYTICS_EVENTS` — `assertValidProps` strips an unlisted key in production and throws on it in dev, so a payload the registry does not name is lost rather than wrong. A case asserts both literals appear in both files: forwarding the argument is worth nothing if both call sites pass `"chip"`, which is what a copy-paste lands on, and the funnel would then look exactly as it did before with a name on it.

  **Third, the sentence now behaves like the control it became yesterday.** It was a 20px-tall line of text with no hit-slop and no pressed state — the shape most likely to feel broken on a phone. `hitSlop={8}` is this tree's existing answer (eight other controls use it) and the pressed style is opacity rather than a colour swap, so the amber that marks it as pressable survives the press. Both halves are asserted, because either alone still leaves it feeling dead.

  `docs/powerbi-connection.md` is regenerated rather than edited — `lint:powerbi-doc` caught the drift the moment the registry gained a prop, which is the guard working. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6145 tests (+9), build. No DB migration, no creds, no new i18n keys.

## The sentence explaining the padlock, which nobody could ever see (2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Locked-chip composed-key follow-ups" — "The upsell fires on the CHIP but not on the visible hint below it". Investigating it found the hint was not merely inert, it was unreachable. Picked deliberately from a user-facing area after three infrastructure tasks. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 17000] **Make the premium-only visibility hint reachable, and make it the same button as the padlocked chip** — the suggestion said a sighted user reads "Private collections are a premium feature" as body text under the visibility row and taps it, and nothing happens. The first half turned out to be wrong in a more interesting way: **the sentence never rendered at all**. Both screens chose the hint with `!isPremium && visibility === "private"`, and a free user can never reach that state — the locked chip calls the upsell and returns BEFORE `setVisibility`. So the branch that explains the padlock was dead in `app/create-collection.tsx` and in `components/edit-collection-modal.tsx`, in all six languages, and the only place the sentence ever appeared was a toast that has gone by the time somebody looks back at the row.

  **What ships:** the hint under the row now always describes the CURRENT selection, and when the private option is locked a second line appears beside it — amber and bold, so it reads as something to press next to a sentence that is not — carrying the premium explanation and firing the same upsell the chip does. Adding a line rather than swapping one: choosing a single sentence for both jobs is exactly what made the explanation unreachable, and replacing "public collections are visible to everyone" with "private is premium" would trade away the useful half for the user who is actually looking at a public collection. The a11y label is the one already written for the locked chip.

  **The rule moved to `lib/premium-helpers.ts`, which is where the drift showed.** Both rows had written the lock out for themselves and the copies already disagreed: create locked on `!isPremium` alone, edit on `!isPremium && (savedVisibility ?? "private") !== "private"`. `isPrivateVisibilityLocked(isPremium, savedVisibility)` is now one rule, and the part that genuinely differs stays at the call site as a decision each screen makes: create passes `"public"` (a collection being created has no private history to protect) and the sheet passes `savedVisibility ?? "private"` (the value can be momentarily unknown while the collection loads, and treating unknown as private errs toward not locking an owner out of their own sheet). Both defaults are asserted, because swapping them is silent — one locks every free user out of nothing, the other unlocks the gate entirely.

  `__tests__/premium-visibility-lock.test.ts` exercises the rule by CALLING it, including the lapsed-subscriber case that was the reason it takes the saved visibility rather than the selected one, and pins one thing the refactor could quietly break: moving the upsell body into a named `showPrivateUpsell()` per screen is exactly where two call sites become one analytics event, so the distinct `source: "create_collection"` / `"collection_edit"` is a case — the standing "hide the locked chip once PostHog shows nobody taps it" decision is waiting on that funnel. Three existing suites pinned the old inline expressions and now pin the new shape; `edit-collection-modal`'s string sweep checks the two per-selection keys one level down, in the helper that names them.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6136 tests (+10), build. No DB migration, no creds, no new i18n keys — this makes copy that was already translated into six languages visible for the first time.

## The scanner that was copied without its careful half (2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Pressable-role follow-ups" — "`interactivePressables` is the fourth open-tag scanner in this repository". Three of the four it named are gone (the sweep suites were deleted when the sweep hit zero), and the run an hour earlier wrote a new one, which is what made the entry actionable rather than historical. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 15000] **Extract `lib/jsx-open-tag.ts` so there is one answer to "where does this tag end"** — the standing suggestion counted four copies of the same JSX open-tag walk and said three handled brace depth identically because they were copied from each other. The count is what changed: the sweep suites that held two of them were deleted when the a11y sweep reached zero, leaving `lib/check-a11y-jsx.ts`'s private version as the only careful one — and then the sheet-Escape task an hour ago wrote a fresh copy that had the brace counter and NOT the string-literal skipping. That is the whole argument for extracting, demonstrated rather than asserted: the reasoning was written down in one file's doc comment, the next person needed the same walk, and the half they left out is the half that is silent when it is wrong.

  **What moved and what deliberately did not.** `skipStringLiteral`, `openTagEnd` and `closeTagIndex` moved verbatim out of the guard into `lib/jsx-open-tag.ts`, keeping the reasoning that came with them — the naive `/<Pressable([\s\S]*?)>/` ends the tag at the first `>` in the file, which here is usually the one inside `onPress={() => x}`, and on the a11y sweep it reported ten offenders where the careful version reports three: seven false positives, every one a button that DID carry a label in attribute text the regex never reached. What stayed in the guard is its tag STACK — tracking ancestors so a node inside a hidden subtree is not asked for props of its own is a rule about that guard's domain, not a primitive. The module is explicitly not a parser: callers ask about a tag they have already located and get back text they still have to interpret.

  Two readers were promoted rather than moved, because the sheet suite needed the inverse question and its versions were the weaker ones. `openTagAt(source, at)` reads back from an attribute offset to the whole tag that carries it; `attributeValue(tag, name)` reads the expression inside `name={…}`, brace- AND string-aware, whitespace flattened so a prop the formatter wrapped across three lines compares equal to the same handler written inline (there is no prettier here, so both spellings genuinely exist). Two decisions in it are stated as cases rather than comments: the name is matched only where a name can START, so asking for `label` does not match `accessibilityLabel`; and the string form (`accessibilityRole="none"`) returns null rather than its text, because a caller that conflated the two would be comparing a value against the expression that produces it. The sheet suite also got more precise in the swap — it now reads the Modal's own open tag instead of its whole span, so an `onRequestClose` on a nested element can no longer stand in for a Modal that has none.

  `__tests__/jsx-open-tag.test.ts` states each mistake the module prevents as its own case, which is what the old arrangement could not do: covered only through the guard's findings, a case here was really a case about `<Pressable>` and `accessibilityLabel`, and the rule ("a `>` inside an attribute does not end the tag") was never said on its own. Eighteen cases: a `>` in an arrow, in a string, in a template; an unterminated tag and an unterminated literal returning the honest answer rather than a guess (these scanners read files mid-edit, and one that threw would turn a lint run into a crash); a nested same-name close tag; the truncated-arrow shape; a brace inside a string; both ends of the name-boundary rule.

  Verified behaviour-preserving where it counts: planting an unlabeled icon-only `<Pressable>` into `components/currency-sheet.tsx` still fails `lint:a11y-jsx` with all three of its codes (`no_role`, `unlabeled`, `undecided_icon`) at the right line, and the tree reports clean again on revert. Worth recording from that check: the first plant landed inside a doc comment and was correctly ignored — the guard blanks comments through `stripComments`, which is a feature working, not a miss. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6126 tests (+18), build. No DB migration, no creds.

## The dependency the a11y argument rests on, and the key that was never compared (2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Verified-negative follow-ups" — two entries from the run that wrote the sheet suite: "`react-native-web`'s modal behaviour is now load-bearing and unpinned" and "Nothing checks that a sheet's `onRequestClose` actually closes it". They are one task because the first is what makes the second's Escape key work at all. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 14000] **Pin what `react-native-web`'s `<Modal>` provides, and check every sheet's Escape agrees with its backdrop** — `sheet-modal-semantics` argues that eleven sheets need no `accessibilityViewIsModal` because they sit inside a `<Modal>`. On the platform most of this app's users are on — the web build on GitHub Pages — "inside a Modal" means whatever `react-native-web` decides it means, and that was read out of `node_modules` by a person once and then written into a doc comment, which is where a fact about a dependency goes to stop being checked. An upgrade that dropped the focus trap would change none of the eleven files and leave nothing looking wrong; the sheets would simply stop being modal for a screen reader, and the suggestion the whole suite exists to refute would become correct at last.

  `__tests__/rnw-modal-premise.test.ts` is the cheap version of what `lint:sentry-version` does for the Sentry SDK: it reads the SHIPPED `exports/Modal/*.js` (0.21.2 today) and asserts the three separate things the argument uses — `"aria-modal": true` and `role: active ? 'dialog'` on the content, `ModalFocusTrap` both imported AND rendered around the content (a trap that ships but is no longer wired is the regression that leaves the file in place and the behaviour gone), and Escape asserted in two halves, the `e.key === 'Escape'` listener and the `onRequestClose()` call, because either alone is a different regression with the same symptom. Every message names the installed version, so a failure points at what to go and read rather than at itself. It fails CLOSED on a missing file — an upgrade that renames or bundles these away is the single likeliest way for the premise to change, and a `try`/`catch` returning `""` would turn all six cases into silent passes.

  **The second half is what that pinning makes assertable.** Escape calls `onRequestClose`; a backdrop tap calls the backdrop's `onPress`. Two props, the same intent, and nothing had ever compared them — a sheet whose keyboard dismissal does something other than its tap dismissal is a small, very confusing bug that ships because both halves look right on their own line. The new case reads both out of every Modal span that contains the sandwich and requires them equal. All eleven agree today, so this ships green; it is the drift that is being stopped. Two readers were needed and both are brace-aware for a concrete reason: `=>` appears in nearly every handler here, so `indexOf(">")` truncates `onPress={() => setOpen(false)}` at `setOpen(false` and would then compare a handler unequal to itself written inline. Whitespace is flattened so the formatter wrapping a prop across three lines does not read as a mismatch.

  Deliberately NOT reported: a backdrop with no `onPress` at all. The wishlist promote sheet is one — Escape closes it, a tap does not. That is an asymmetry rather than a contradiction, and a rule that fires on it would be exempted and then stopped being read. A vacuity case guards the pair, because the comparison passes trivially if both readers return null everywhere: at least ten files must yield BOTH handlers.

  Mutation-checked both ways rather than assumed. Pointing `components/currency-sheet.tsx`'s `onRequestClose` at a different function fails naming the file and both handlers; flipping `"aria-modal": true` to `false` inside the installed `ModalContent.js` fails naming the version and what it costs a screen reader. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6108 tests (+8), build. No source change — this task's product is two premises that can no longer rot quietly. No DB migration, no creds.

## The public ID that changed every time the screen recomputed (2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Slug-walk follow-ups" — two entries that turned out to be one finding: "the wall-clock suffix is still 13 digits and now has a seam to fix it behind" and "the uniqueness walks are O(n) per candidate". Chasing the first found a live bug the entry did not mention. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 16000] **Seed the anonymous profile-ID suffix on the account ID instead of the wall clock, and make both uniqueness walks linear** — the suggestion asked for a shorter suffix because `collector-1755823419233` is an identifier the app asks a person to SHARE. The length is the smaller half. `String(Date.now())` returns a DIFFERENT value on every call, and both call sites that reach it sit inside the `profiles` memo in `lib/social-context.tsx` — which recomputes on a language change, on an arriving friend request, on any change to the profile list. So a user whose display name and email local part are both non-ASCII (a Cyrillic name with a Cyrillic address is not exotic here — `ru`, `be` and `pl` are three of the six shipped languages) was handed a **new public ID every recompute**. Nobody could find them by the ID on their own screen, and whichever value happened to be current at save time is what `upsertMyProfile` wrote into the cloud row.

  **The fix keeps the one property the clock had.** `slugSuffixFromSeed` is FNV-1a over a seed, truncated to 30 bits and rendered base-36, so it is exactly six characters and always inside `[a-z0-9]` — the alphabet `slugifyProfileId` would otherwise strip, and the suffix is appended AFTER slugification, so a `-`, `_` or `+` in the token would survive unstripped into a URL. `stableSuffix(user.id)` is passed at all three call sites (`buildFallbackProfile` and both walks in `updateMyProfile`), and `normalizeProfile` defaults its suffix to one seeded on the profile's own `id`, which is what makes the function deterministic for every profile the app holds rather than only for the signed-in one. Two accounts with equally unslugifiable names still get different IDs — that is the collision the clock existed to prevent — and `ensureUniquePublicId` resolves the ~1-in-1e9 case where they do not. The hash is one-way on purpose: the seed is the auth UUID and the output goes in a shared URL, so a case asserts no chunk of the account ID appears in the result.

  **Existing users are untouched**, which is why there is no migration: `normalizeProfile` prefers a stored `publicId`, so anybody who has ever saved a profile keeps theirs. The change only reaches profiles that had nothing to slugify — the ones whose ID was never stable in the first place.

  **The walks, in the same pass.** Both re-scanned the whole profile list on every iteration (`profiles.some(...)` inside a `while`), so a name shared by k people cost k full passes. `takenSlugs` reads the list once into a `Set` and `walkToFreeSlug` walks it — identical answers, because neither walk mutates the list. Three cases guard the rewrite where it could differ rather than where it is obvious: a 200-collision run (an off-by-one at scale), a profile whose slug is `""` (normal here — `coerceProfileRow` turns every NULL column into `""`), and a self id appearing on more than one row (the shape that catches a `find`-style rewrite of the exclusion).

  `__tests__/profile-slug-uniqueness.test.ts` grew four describes, including the regression stated as directly as it can be: normalize one profile twice, no injected suffix, assert the two public IDs are equal. With the clock underneath, that failed whenever the two calls landed in different milliseconds. The URL-safety case pins the constraint the two slug alphabets exist to satisfy — `encodeURIComponent(id) === id` over a battery of hostile names — where it can be checked, rather than as a comparison against `slugifyUsername`, which is a second function that could drift the same way. `__tests__/fallback-profile-locale.test.ts` had pinned the old call text exactly and now pins the new one, seed argument included, because omitting it is precisely how the wall clock comes back. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6100 tests (+13), build. No DB migration, no creds.

## The suggestion that was wrong, and the invariant that made it wrong (2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md — "Sheet-sandwich follow-ups" and "Pressable-role follow-ups", where the same claim was filed three times by three different runs. Picked deliberately from a different area than the last six tasks. The outcome is not the one the entry asked for. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 12000] **Do NOT add `accessibilityViewIsModal` to the sheets — verify the claim first, then pin what makes it unnecessary** — the standing suggestion, filed three times, read: "`accessibilityViewIsModal` is set on none of the nine — a sheet is drawn over the screen and a screen reader can still walk out of it into the content behind. That is the actual modal bug; the role work does nothing about focus escaping." It is wrong, and the reason it is wrong is instructive: all three filings were written while sweeping `accessibilityRole` across the JSX of the sandwich, and none of them looked at what the sandwich is nested IN.

  Every sheet in this tree renders inside React Native's `<Modal>` — eleven files, checked one at a time. A `<Modal>` is not a positioned `<View>` on any of the three platforms: iOS presents a separate view controller, which UIKit scopes VoiceOver to; Android renders a Dialog, which is its own window for TalkBack; and `react-native-web` — read in `node_modules` rather than assumed, which is the step that settled it — renders `ModalContent` with `aria-modal: true` and `role="dialog"`, wraps the children in `ModalFocusTrap` (it loops Tab inside the modal and pulls focus back when it escapes), and closes on Escape. So the prop the suggestion asked for would have been dead weight in eleven files, and "the Android half" it called the harder problem does not exist either.

  **What shipped instead is the property that makes it unnecessary, asserted.** `__tests__/sheet-modal-semantics.test.ts` finds every sheet by its fingerprint — the two `accessibilityRole="none"` opt-outs no other shape in this tree uses twice in one file — and requires each to sit inside a `<Modal>` span. The day somebody builds a sheet as a bare absolutely-positioned overlay, which has no focus trap, no Escape-to-close and no `aria-modal`, the suggestion becomes correct and a named case says so rather than nobody noticing. Two more cases guard the guard: the opt-outs must come in PAIRS (a backdrop and a card; an odd count means one of them has quietly become a control again), and a case that fails if `accessibilityViewIsModal` is ever added to a sheet — which exists precisely so the suggestion is not implemented a fourth time by somebody who reads the entry and not this note. The `modalSpans` helper fails CLOSED on a nesting it does not expect: a too-small span reports a sandwich as outside a Modal rather than waving one through.

  Also pinned: the walk finds at least ten files. A sweep that matched nothing would make every per-file case vacuously true, which is the failure the whole repository's floors exist for and the easiest one to ship in a suite like this.

  Mutation-checked both ways. Rewriting `components/currency-sheet.tsx`'s `<Modal>` into a `<View>` fails naming the file and what a positioned overlay costs; adding the redundant prop to the same sheet fails the case written to stop it. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6087 tests (+15), build. No source change at all — this task's product is a verified negative and a test. No DB migration, no creds.

## The rule for the leak, not just the fix for it (2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Public-ID privacy follow-ups", top entry — filed an hour earlier by the run that fixed the leak itself, which is the right split: the fix was one removed term and belonged in its own review; the rule that stops the next one is a different change with its own registration surface. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 19000] **`check-profile-id-pii`: fail when an email address reaches a public profile identifier** — the previous task removed one `|| profile.email` from `normalizeProfile`'s `publicId` chain, and nothing stopped the next one. This is the sixteenth guard, written the moment the count reached zero, which is the only time a rule like this can be written — the same pattern `check-a11y-jsx`'s `no_role` and `undecided_icon` followed, and the reason the standing suggestion said the orphan guard had to wait for the families to be removed first.

  **Which fields, and the one deliberately left out.** `publicId` / `public_id` and `username` are covered: both are identifiers a person shares, is searched by, and does not choose at sign-up, and both are written back to the cloud row so a bad value sticks. `displayName` is NOT covered, and that is the decision worth recording: deriving a display name from an email's local part is INTENDED here — somebody signing up as `ada@example.com` should be called "ada" rather than "Collector", and `resolveFallbackIdentity` does exactly that. A rule covering `displayName` would fire on live, reviewed code, and a rule that fires on correct code is one somebody exempts and then stops reading. The exclusion is a case in the suite rather than only a comment.

  **The seam, which is the interesting part of the rule.** It matches `email` as a WHOLE WORD, so `emailName` and `userEmail` pass. That is not a gap it fails to close — it is the line it is drawing. An address's local part does legitimately seed a username, and in this tree it does, via `emailName` → `slugSeed`: a named intermediate somebody had to write down and a reviewer can see. What the rule is actually about is a raw address reaching an identifier INLINE, where nobody has weighed which part of it survives — which is exactly the shape the bug had.

  Two implementation details that came from the bug rather than from taste. The value reader is brace- and quote-aware because the offending expression spanned three lines inside a call whose own arguments were comma-separated: both "read to the next comma" and "read to end of line" truncate it before the term that mattered, so a simpler scanner would have reported the tree clean. And the planted-offender fixture in `guard-report-paths` is multi-line for the same reason — a single-line plant would be found by a scanner this guard has already outgrown.

  Registered the full way rather than as a suite: `LINT_GUARDS`, `SCANNED_FLOORS` (floor 200 over the same five-root walk `check-orphan-i18n-keys` takes, 264 files, riding above the 165 `lib/` alone contributes — and `lib/` is where every profile-shaping function lives, so a walk that had quietly lost it would report a clean tree while reading none of the code the rule is about), `package.json`, plus the two registry meta-guards: a planted-offender case in `guard-report-paths` and a partial-root fixture in `lint-guard-partial-root`. CI needs no change because it runs `lint:all`.

  Verified end to end against the real thing, which is the only verification that means anything here: putting `|| profile.email` back into `lib/social-helpers.ts` exits 1 and prints `lib/social-helpers.ts:357  publicId: slugifyProfileId( profile.publicId || … || profile.email, suffix, )`, and the tree reports clean again when it is reverted. 13 cases in the guard's own suite, built around the two things the bug proved it has to get right. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 16/16, 6072 tests (+30), build. No DB migration, no creds.

## The email address that became a public profile ID (2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Slug-walk follow-ups" — the entry noted that `normalizeProfile` was the last pure function behind the React Native import wall AND that it had "a privacy-shaped decision sitting in a `||` chain". Extracting it was the task; the chain turned out to be a live leak. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 18000] **Stop deriving a public profile ID from the email address, and extract the last function behind the wall** — `normalizeProfile`'s `publicId` fallback chain read `profile.publicId || profile.username || profile.displayName || profile.email`. That last term is the finding, and it is reachable rather than theoretical: `coerceProfileRow` in `lib/supabase-row-coerce.ts` turns every NULL column into `""`, so a cloud row with no `public_id`, no `username` and no `display_name` but a real address arrives as a profile whose only non-empty name is the email — and comes out with `ada-lovelace-example-com` as its public ID.

  What makes it worth a task rather than a note: `publicId` is not internal. It is the identifier the app invites people to SHARE so others can find them (the removed `profileIdHint` key read "Use this ID so other people can find your profile"), `lib/supabase-profiles-shapes.ts` writes it to the cloud row as `public_id` on every `upsertMyProfile`, and it is in `PROFILE_COLUMNS` on read. So an address-derived ID does not stay local — it is written back, shared, and searchable, and both the local part and the domain survive slugification. The chain now ends at the display name; a profile with nothing to name it gets the anonymous seed, which is what `slugifyProfileId` already does with an empty string, so the fix removed a term rather than adding a branch.

  `normalizeProfile` moved to `lib/social-helpers.ts` in the same pass, which is the reason the branch had never been exercised: the function sat behind React Native peers `tsx --test` cannot transform, so nothing had ever called it. It is the last of the four extracted this evening. Two behaviours were deliberately preserved and are pinned so a later tidy has to argue with them: the `username` fallback is the bare seed with NO suffix (several nameless profiles share the username `collector`; adding a suffix would rename profiles already stored under it, and `ensureUniqueUsername` is what resolves a clash when somebody actually picks a name), and the `publicId` preference order — chosen ID, then username, then display name — is asserted at each step rather than only at the end.

  **The remediation is a data fix and it is written down rather than done.** The code stops producing these IDs; rows written before it still carry whatever was derived earlier. `MANUAL-TASKS.md` gains a section with a read-only SELECT that finds affected rows by comparing each `public_id` against its own email slugified the same way the app did, and a deliberately unqualified-refusing UPDATE. It says out loud not to blank these silently: a cleared `public_id` breaks any profile link already handed out, so contacting the affected users and letting them pick a new ID is the preferred path and clearing is for unused rows.

  Mutation-checked on the leak itself, both shapes: restoring `|| profile.email` fails 2 cases naming the fragment that escaped ("'ada' from the email address reached the public profile ID: ada-lovelace-example-com"), and so does the careless partial fix that leaks only the local part — which is why that case asserts the fragments one at a time rather than comparing the whole string against the seed. Two wiring assertions from the previous two tasks pointed at `lib/social-context.tsx` and were repointed rather than deleted, since the functions have been migrating between the two files all evening; the "no hand-written copy of the fallback word" claim now checks BOTH files. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 15/15, 6042 tests (+5), build. No schema migration; one data remediation, documented. No creds.

## Two loops nobody had ever run (2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Pure-extraction follow-ups" — the top entry, filed by the run below after it extracted the first pure function out of the same file and noticed three more behind the same wall. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 14000] **Extract `slugifyProfileId`, `ensureUniquePublicId` and `ensureUniqueUsername`, and give their collision paths a first test** — all three sat in `lib/social-context.tsx`, behind React Native peers `tsx --test` cannot transform, so nothing had ever executed them. That includes two `while` loops that walk `-2`, `-3`, … looking for a free slug — untested at any suffix — and a last-resort branch that could not be reached deterministically at all, because it stamped `Date.now()`.

  The clock is an injected `UniqueSuffix` now, defaulted to the wall clock so no call site changes, which gives the branch a fixed value to assert. It is not configuration anybody wants and the doc comment says so. The `profiles` argument became a structural `{ id, publicId?, username? }` rather than `UserProfile`, so a fixture is three fields instead of eight and the walks cannot quietly start depending on something else about a profile. `slugifyUsername` came out of `ensureUniqueUsername`, where it had been inlined — the two slugifiers stay separate functions rather than one with a separator parameter, because a public ID appears in a URL and a username does not, and a case pins that they disagree on both the separator and on what happens to an underscore that was already there.

  **The case worth having most** is the `selfId` guard: without it, re-saving a profile without changing its ID reads as a collision with itself, so editing a bio would walk somebody from `ada-lovelace` to `ada-lovelace-2` and again on every save. It had no test. It has two now — one asserting the guard holds, one asserting the same call WITHOUT `selfId` does renumber, so the case cannot pass by the guard being unreachable. A second deliberate behaviour is pinned because the obvious alternative is so tempting: the walk stops at the first FREE suffix rather than the highest taken one, so a deleted `-2` is handed out again.

  Related tidy in the same pass: `normalizeProfile`'s `|| "collector"` now reads the shared `FALLBACK_SLUG_SEED`. Four paths invent a name for a nameless profile and they agreed by three separate hand-written literals; the agreement is structural now, and the previous task's own wiring case — which pinned the two literals — was updated rather than deleted, since what it is really about is that the constant is imported and not re-typed. It caught this refactor, correctly, which is what it was for.

  Mutation-checked four ways, all of them things no previous test could have seen because the functions were unreachable: disabling the public-ID collision loop fails 5 cases; freezing its counter hangs the run (`timeout` exits 124 — an infinite loop IS the failure); pointing the username walk at `publicId`, the copy-paste bug the two near-identical loops invite, fails 3; dropping the `selfId` guard fails 1. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 15/15, 6037 tests (+20), build. No DB migration, no creds.

## Tests that read the source, made to call the code (2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Fallback-profile follow-ups" — filed by the run directly below against its own suite, which is the honest place for it: the suite it shipped was the best available at the time and it knew what was wrong with it. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 13000] **Extract `resolveFallbackIdentity` into `lib/social-helpers.ts` so the fallback chain is tested by being CALLED** — `lib/social-context.tsx` pulls React Native peers, and `tsx --test` cannot transform them (`Unexpected "typeof"` out of `react-native/index.js`), so every assertion about `buildFallbackProfile` was a regex over its source text. That is a real weakness and not a stylistic one: a refactor that keeps the text and breaks the behaviour passes every one of them, and the suite would go green while a user with no email address got an empty name.

  The pure part moved: the `full_name` → email-local-part → translated-default chain, the ASCII slug seed, and the placeholder address, all in a function that takes a plain `{ email, fullName, userName }` and the localised default. `lib/social-helpers.ts` was already the right home — it imports nothing but types and is node-testable, which is what the suggestion pointed at. What deliberately did NOT move is `slugifyProfileId`: its last-resort branch stamps `Date.now()` so two profiles with unslugifiable names cannot collide, which makes it non-deterministic for exactly the inputs that reach it (a non-ASCII email local part), and dragging it into a pure module would have meant either threading a clock through or losing the uniqueness. The split is at the honest seam — the function returns `slugSeed` and the caller slugifies.

  **A bug found by writing the first real test.** An address with an empty local part — `"@example.com"` — split to `""`, which is falsy but was being passed through `??`, so it became an empty display name AND an empty username. `""` is not a name; it is now treated as no address at all and falls through to the translated default and the ASCII seed. Nine source-text assertions became calls with real inputs, and this is the case none of them could have expressed.

  Mutation-checked two ways, both chosen because the OLD suite would have passed them: seeding the slug from the translated display name (the exact regression the split exists to prevent) turns two cases red, and dropping the email local part out of the display-name chain turns two red. Both mutations live in `lib/social-helpers.ts`, a file the old suite never read at all — which is the argument for the extraction in one sentence.

  The source-text assertions that remain are the ones genuinely about WIRING rather than behaviour, and they are kept for that reason: that `buildFallbackProfile` actually calls the resolver, that it slugifies `identity.slugSeed`, that the bio sentinel is exported from the writer and imported by the screen, and that `?? "you"` has not come back. A pure function can be perfect and unused. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 15/15, 6017 tests (+5), build. No DB migration, no creds.

## The English word under the dead key (2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Key-position-rule follow-ups" — filed by the run directly below, which found it while deleting the dead `you` key and deliberately did NOT fix it in that change: a translation-key removal and a behaviour fix are two different reviews. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 16000] **Translate the fallback profile's display name, and give the bio sentinel one definition instead of two** — deleting the orphan `you` yesterday was a cleanup; the literal underneath it was a live bug. `buildFallbackProfile` in `lib/social-context.tsx` builds the profile a person sees after sign-up and offline, and it fell back to the English word `"you"` as their NAME when the session carried no email address and no `full_name` metadata — in whatever language the app was in. It reads a new `defaultDisplayName` now, declared AND translated in all six locales (Collector / Коллекционер / Калекцыянер / Kolekcjoner / Sammler / Coleccionista), and the email's local part still wins ahead of it so somebody with an address is still called by the name in it.

  **The interesting half is what was deliberately NOT translated.** The old `baseName` fed three fields, and two of them are SLUGS: `username` and `publicId` go through `slugifyProfileId`, which strips everything outside `[a-z0-9]`. Translating the seed would have left a Russian or Belarusian user with an empty slug rescued by the timestamp fallback into `collector-1755...` — a worse profile ID than they get today, and a regression dressed as a fix. The slug seed is now an explicit `FALLBACK_SLUG_SEED = "collector"`, which is the same word `normalizeProfile` and `ensureUniqueUsername` already fall back to, so the three paths finally agree instead of each inventing their own. A case asserts the seed is a constant and not the translated name, so a future pass "finishing the job" by translating the other fields fails loudly.

  **Second finding, in the same function:** `bio` was a hardcoded English sentence — and it turned out to be a second copy of `DEFAULT_EN_PROFILE_BIO`, a private `const` in `app/profile/[id].tsx` that the screen compares the stored bio against in order to translate it on render. Two copies of one sentence in two files with nothing tying them together: rewording either would have left the screen comparing against a sentence nothing writes any more, and the symptom would have been a bio that quietly stopped translating rather than an error. The constant is exported from the writer and imported by the reader now. It is deliberately STILL written in English, which is the counter-intuitive half and the reason the two fields got different fixes: the bio is persisted — to AsyncStorage and to the cloud row other people read — so localising it at write time would freeze it in the author's language, and a Polish viewer would read Russian. The display name is safe to localise because it is recomputed on every render until the person edits their profile, at which point they have chosen a name of their own. Both halves are pinned, including a case asserting `bio: t("defaultProfileBio")` does NOT appear, with the reason in the message.

  A related trap the suite had to work around rather than solve: `lib/social-context.tsx` pulls React Native peers, so `tsx --test` cannot import it (`Unexpected "typeof"` out of `react-native/index.js`). The sentinel's value is read back out of the committed source instead — the same technique `i18n-translation-coverage` uses on the translations file, and it checks the text the profile screen actually compares against at runtime. A case pins that the `en` translation and the sentinel are byte-identical, because the comparison is `===` and one character of drift silently disables the render-time translation.

  Base count 501 (500 + `defaultDisplayName`) — the first key this file has GAINED in a week of removals, and it arrived because a removal uncovered the bug. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 15/15, 6012 tests (+12), build. No DB migration, no creds.

## The guard's own blind spot, measured — and the key that was hiding in it (2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Orphan-guard follow-ups" — a suggestion the previous run filed AGAINST ITS OWN guard, which is the reason it was worth doing next: an unmeasured limit in a rule that shipped an hour earlier is the cheapest thing in the file to be wrong about. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 15000] **Measure the orphan guard's loose direction, close it, and delete the 37th key it was hiding** — `check-orphan-i18n-keys` shipped with the rule "a base key is read if it appears anywhere in the source tree as a string literal", and shipped with a follow-up saying that rule counted a key as read whenever its name collided with an unrelated literal, that nobody had measured how big that hole was, and that if it turned out small, naming the occupants would be cheaper than tightening the rule. Measured: **the blind spot had exactly one occupant, and it was a real orphan.**

  `you` — "You", and translated into `ru`, `be`, `pl`, `de` and `es` as well — is rendered by nothing. `t("you")` appears in no file in the repository. Its only mention anywhere in the source tree is `const baseName = user.email?.split("@")[0] ?? "you"` in `lib/social-context.tsx`, a default display name derived from an email local-part that has nothing whatever to do with the key. It is the only one of the 37 dead keys that somebody translated into all six languages, so five people's worth of work went into a string with no consumer — the "dead keys inflate every coverage row" point the earliest suggestion in this thread made, with the worst case of it attached to a name.

  **The measurement changed the answer.** The suggestion offered "name the occupants" as the cheap option; one occupant made tightening cheaper still, and the tightened rule is more honest besides. A mention now counts only when the literal sits in a POSITION a translation key is written in: after `(` or `,` (an argument — `t("k")`, `pick(t, "k")`), after `:` (a record value — `from_negative: "k"`, `labelKey: "k"`), after `as` or `|` (a union member). The last of those is load-bearing and was found by measuring rather than by reasoning: three of the four `condition*` keys are spelled as wrapped continuation lines (`| "conditionExcellent"`) in `components/item-card.tsx`, so a rule demanding `as "k"` on one line would have reported three live keys. A fallback value is preceded by `?`, and qualifies under none of the five. Over the whole tree the tightened rule reports one orphan and zero false positives; the loose rule reported none and missed it. Still no exemptions list.

  Counts: base 501 → 500, `ru` 500 → 498, and every partial locale drops one (be 233 → 232, pl 224 → 223, de 220 → 219, es 221 → 220) — the only one of the 37 to touch all six rows. All clear of the floors re-measured earlier today. `REMOVED_FAMILIES` gains a fourth entry, a family of one: `you` belongs to none of the three sweeps, and the note records that it is the key that proved the blind spot was worth measuring rather than merely documenting.

  Verified end to end on the real tree, which is what the fixtures cannot do: planting a `plantedValueKey` whose ONLY mention is `?? "plantedValueKey"` in `lib/social-context.tsx` exits 1 and names it — the case the shipped rule would have waved through. Six new cases in the guard's suite, one per branch of the position alternation plus the three non-positions (`=`, `===`, `return`) and the wrapped-union line. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 15/15, 6000 tests (+5), build. No DB migration, no creds.

## The scan that found three dead families, made permanent — and the key it found that the scans missed (2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Orphan-scan follow-ups", filed by the run directly below. The standing entry had said for three sessions that this guard should land only once the orphan count was near zero, because a rule floored at 35 findings means nothing. Removing the third family took the count to zero, which is what unblocked it. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 22000] **`check-orphan-i18n-keys`: make the throwaway scan a guard, and delete the 36th orphan it found on its first run** — three families and 35 keys were removed this week by a tsx in scratchpad that died with the container each time, and nothing stopped the next dead key being added. The guard is `scripts/check-orphan-i18n-keys.ts` + `lib/check-orphan-i18n-keys.ts` (the repo's wrapper/pure-module split), registered in `LINT_GUARDS`, `SCANNED_FLOORS` and `package.json` as `lint:orphan-i18n` — 15 guards now, and CI needs no change because it runs `lint:all`.

  **The rule is not `t("…")`, and that is the whole design.** Matching the direct call is the obvious answer and it is measurably wrong here: 43 of the tree's 502 base keys are never written inside a `t(...)` call at all. They reach the translator through four different forms — the direct call; a key stored in a table (`{ mode: "name-asc", labelKey: "sortNameAsc" }` and `from_negative: "filterPriceNegative"` in `lib/item-filters.ts`, `empty: "currencyErrorEmpty"` in `lib/format-currency-input.ts`); a helper that takes the key (`pick(t, "crashFallbackTitle")` in `components/crash-fallback.tsx`); and the `as` clause of the one dynamic build. So the rule is "the key appears anywhere in the source tree as a bare string literal", which covers all four uniformly. That is also what makes the EXEMPTIONS LIST the original suggestion asked for unnecessary, and the reason is worth keeping: the dynamic build's `as` clause is itself a list of string literals, so the hard case the design worried about — a hand-maintained allowlist for dynamically-built keys, drifting out of date — does not arise. The rule is loose in one direction (a key colliding with an unrelated literal counts as read) and that is the stated price of having nothing to justify twice.

  **It found `profileId` immediately, and three hand scans had not.** The scratch scans matched `\bkey\b` over the tree, and `lib/social-context.tsx` declares six `(profileId: string)` PARAMETERS — bare-word matching cannot tell a parameter name from a key reference, so the key looked read. It is dead: it is the LABEL of the never-built "Save profile ID" field whose `profileIdHint` and `saveProfileId` went with the social/people family a day earlier, and the only sibling any screen renders is `profileIdPlaceholder`. Removed, `en`/`ru` only, base 502 → 501 and `ru` 500 → 499, both clear of the floors re-measured in the previous task. `REMOVED_FAMILIES` grows to 16 in the social family and 36 overall.

  **Knock-on fixed rather than worked around:** adding `profileId` to `dead-i18n-keys.test.ts` turned its own source-tree case red, because that case still used `\bkey\b` — the same looseness that hid the key. It now matches through `collectStringLiterals`, the guard's exported rule, so "the guard says this key is dead" and "this suite says it stays dead" can no longer be answered differently. One rule, two consumers.

  **Two registry meta-guards caught the incomplete registration and both were satisfied properly, not exempted.** `guard-report-paths` requires every `listSourceFiles` caller to have a planted-offender case; this guard reports no `path:line:column` at all, because its finding is an ABSENCE and there is no location to name. That is a categorically different exemption from the existing one (which is about fixture cost), so it gets its own `REPORTS_NO_PATH` list plus a case that asserts the REASON — the report must contain no path-shaped token — so the day the guard learns to name a file, the exemption fails instead of quietly covering it. `lint-guard-partial-root` requires a red run for every count floor; `["app"]` at 19 files against a floor of 200 is the fixture, and the note records why the order matters: `assertScannedFloor` runs BEFORE the guard reads `lib/i18n-context.tsx`, so a partial root refuses on `below_floor` rather than on a missing file.

  Floor 200 with the property the other multi-root notes name: it rides above the 163 `lib/` alone contributes, so no single root clears it. That matters more here than for most guards, because a key's only reader is often a table in `lib/` rather than a `t()` call in a screen — a walk that had quietly lost `app/` would report those keys live while missing every orphan a screen used to render.

  Verified end to end, both directions: a planted `plantedDeadKey` in the base map exits 1 naming it and its locales; a `plantedTableKey` read ONLY through a table entry in `lib/item-filters.ts` exits 0. 13 cases in `__tests__/check-orphan-i18n-keys.test.ts`, built around the four reference forms rather than the direct call. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 15/15, 5995 tests (+31), build. No DB migration, no creds.

## The discover feed that was never built, and the floor the last family owed (2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Dead-i18n-key follow-ups" — the third and last of the families the orphan scan named, and the one the standing entry said would own the `TRANSLATION_FLOORS` re-measure. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 16000] **Delete the last ten dead i18n keys, re-measure all six translation floors, and take the orphan count to zero** — the feed/share family. THREE lost their call sites in the same 2026-04-12 / 2026-05-01 rewrites the first two families traced to (`tabDiscover`, `needMoreDataItemText`, `needTitleText`); SEVEN were never rendered in any commit — an empty discover feed with its hint and call to action (`emptyFeedTitle`, `emptyFeedHint`, `discoverCta`), a share-with-users sheet with its hint and empty state (`shareWithUsers`, `shareWithUsersHint`, `noUsersToShare`), and `conditionPlaceholder`. That last one is the only near-miss of the whole exercise and it was read at the call site rather than trusted to the scan: the three dynamic `t()` builds in the tree spell their union out — `as "conditionNew" | "conditionExcellent" | "conditionGood" | "conditionFair"` — and `conditionPlaceholder` is not in it. The reasoning is recorded beside the key in the table, because the next person to see a `condition*` name in a delete list will have the same doubt.

  **The floor re-measure, which is what made this family different.** All ten were declared in `en` and `ru` only, so the four partial locales lost nothing and `ru` went 510 → 500 — exactly its committed floor. The case that caught it is not the one the standing suggestion predicted: "'ru' has not lost translations" (`declared >= minimum`) PASSES at 500 >= 500, and the failure comes from the slack case instead, reading "'ru' floor sits at or above the current count, so ordinary churn — one deleted English key removing a translated one with it — turns it red". That is the better message of the two and it is exactly right, so the worry filed in the previous entry (that equality would read as "below the floor") was wrong and is corrected here. All six floors are re-measured against the current tree, each keeping the slack policy ITS OWN note states rather than being flattened to one number: `en` 500 → 466 and `ru` 500 → 465 at ~7% (the base map and the maintained locale, the tightest of the six), and be/pl/de/es 205/197/193/193 → 214/206/202/203 at ~8%, "roughly one feature's worth of keys". The four partial notes also record why their PERCENTAGES rose without a word being translated — the denominator shrank by 35 while they lost at most 4 declarations — which is the arithmetic that would otherwise read as progress. Mutation-checked by putting `ru` back to 500: the slack case goes red naming the locale.

  **Found by the guard I had just written, and fixed rather than exempted:** the first draft of the `es` floor note explained the locale's one-key lead by naming `openedForYou`, a key removed in the previous task. `TRANSLATION_FLOORS` notes are string literals, not comments, so `sourceCode` does not strip them and the "nothing in the source tree names a removed key" case reported `lib/i18n-coverage.ts: openedForYou` — correctly, since no scanner can tell prose in a string from a reader of the key. The note now says "the 'opened for you' header" and keeps its meaning. Loosening the rule to allow the mention was the other option and it was the wrong one: the rule is worth more sharp than the sentence was worth verbatim.

  `REMOVED_FAMILIES` is complete at three families and 35 keys, with a new case asserting both numbers together — the existing per-key count would still have passed on a table that lost a whole family, and 35 is the finding the three tasks share. **The orphan count is now zero: every one of the 502 base keys has a reader.** That is the precondition the standing suggestion set for the orphan GUARD (its floor starts at zero rather than at 35), so that task is now unblocked and filed at the top of the suggestions.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 14/14, 5964 tests (+2), build. No DB migration, no creds.

## Fifteen strings for screens that were rewritten, and six for a UI nobody built (2026-08-21)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Dead-i18n-key follow-ups" — the top entry, and the family the suggestion's second bullet asked to trace before deleting. - [DONE: 2026-08-21] [est: 18000] **Delete the fifteen dead social/people i18n keys, and give the third absence guard the helper the second one earned** — an orphan scan over the base map (rerun here, 35 orphans, matching the suggestion's count exactly) confirmed no reader in `app/`, `components/`, `lib/`, `scripts/`, `data/`, `__tests__` or `docs/`, and no dynamic `t(\`prefix${x}\`)` build spells them — the only dynamic constructor in the tree is the `condition*` one, which touches none of these. The suggestion's second bullet asked for `git log -S` per key before deleting, and it needed the clone un-shallowed (50 commits of 892) to answer; the answer splits the family in two and the split is the interesting part. NINE had real call sites, added at `init` and deleted in `8b35e1b` on 2026-04-12 when `app/people.tsx` was rewritten and `app/friends.tsx` was added — `peopleTitle`, `peopleSubtitle`, `noPeopleFound`, `loadingPeople`, `noFriendsYet`, `noFriendsYetTab`, `noRequestsYet`, `noFollowingYet`, `subscribeToSeeCollections` — ordinary leftovers. SIX never had a call site in any commit: `friendBadge`, `followingBadge`, `invitationSent`, `searchFilterByOwner`, `profileIdHint`, `saveProfileId` — copy for a friend/following badge on a profile row, a "By owner" search filter and a "Save profile ID" field with its hint, none of which was ever built. `invitationSent` is the one of the fifteen translated into all six locales, so four languages carried a translation for a string nobody ever rendered; the suggestion's "dead keys inflate every denominator" point, with a name on it. Filed as a suggestion below rather than left implicit, which is what the promoting entry asked for.

  Counts: base 537 → 522, `ru` 535 → 520 (both clear of their floor of 500 — `en` and `ru` declared all fifteen), and be/pl/de/es drop one each with `invitationSent` (237/228/224/224 → 236/227/223/223, floors 205/197/193/193). No `TRANSLATION_FLOORS` re-measurement, which is exactly why this family went first: the two still outstanding (collections-list 10, feed/share 10) land `ru` at 500 against a strictly-greater floor, so whichever is removed last owns that re-measure. `i18n-translation-coverage.test.ts`'s "publishes the honest number" moved to `en: 522/522` with the reason prepended to its note; `ru`'s inherited list stays at two (a brand and an email address) because `ru` DECLARED all fifteen, so removing them adds nothing to the inherited set.

  The guard is the third of its shape, which the suggestion named as the point to stop copying the loop: `assertNoLocaleDeclares(source, predicate, why)` now sits in `__tests__/helpers/i18n-locales.ts` beside `assertDeclaredInEveryLocale` as its mirror, and both hand-rolled loops moved onto it — `visibility-chip-a11y.test.ts`'s `visibilityShared` case and `runtime-config-i18n-forbidden.test.ts`'s `runtimeConfig*` case. A predicate rather than a key because the three claims are three shapes (an exact name, a prefix, a set of fifteen), and the part worth writing once is the ANSWER: every offending `locale: key` pair listed. The realistic regression is a removal that reaches `en` and misses `ru` — the inheriting locales serve English either way, so nothing at runtime shows it — and that is what the message names. Deliberately reads DECLARED keys rather than resolved ones: a key only `en` declares resolves for `ru` through its `...en` spread, and reporting `ru` for it would make the failure unfixable, because there is nothing in the `ru` map to delete. Pinned by a case.

  New `__tests__/dead-social-i18n-keys.test.ts` (3 cases) pins both halves of the removal, because re-adding the key is only half of a reintroduction: the absence in every locale map, AND that nothing in the source tree names one of the fifteen — through `sourceCode`, so a comment recording why a key went is not itself an offence, and over the source tree only, since this suite writes all fifteen names out. Mutation-checked both ways: reviving `peopleTitle` in the `ru` map turns both cases red naming `ru: peopleTitle`, and planting a `"peopleTitle"` reference in `app/people.tsx` with no declaration turns the second red naming `app/people.tsx:peopleTitle`. Five cases added to `i18n-locales-helper.test.ts` for the new helper, including the half-removed fixture and the mirror-of-the-declaration-check pair.

  `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 14/14, 5960 tests (5961 before — twelve per-locale cases collapsed into three helper calls whose messages name the locale, eleven added), build. No DB migration, no creds.
