Distinct from the `visibilityShared` guard because this one is about a whole forbidden feature's namespace, not a single contradictory key. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 14/14, 5961 tests (+6), build. No DB migration, no creds. Remaining orphan families (social/people, collections-list, feed/share — ~35 keys, the ones that DO cross the `ru` floor) stay filed as the follow-up below.

## The visibility key that read "Shared" in one language and "Public" in another (2026-08-20)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, top entry of "Visibility-text translation follow-ups": `visibilityShared` was mismatched at the source (en "Shared", ru "Публичная"/Public) with the four other locales inheriting the English one, so which meaning a user saw depended on where it was rendered. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-20] [est: 7000] **Remove the dead, self-contradictory `visibilityShared` key** — the entry read "Shared" in `en` and "Публичная" (Public) in `ru`, a contradiction at the source, and the other four locales inherited the English one. The suggestion's first step was to read each call site and decide which of the two meanings `visibilityShared` was for — but there are no call sites: the key is rendered by nothing. `components/visibility-badge.tsx` reads `visibilityViewer` ("Shared with you") for a collection someone shared with me and `visibilityPublic` for a public one; `visibilityShared` is a third, unused name that duplicated `visibilityViewer` while disagreeing with itself across languages. Because nothing renders it, no runtime assertion could ever have caught the contradiction — `t("visibilityShared")` returns a non-empty string in all six locales — so the honest fix is to delete the two declarations rather than invent a meaning for a string with no consumer. Removing it drops the base count 547 → 546 (and the be/pl/de/es inherited denominators by one, floors untouched — those gate `declared`, which is unchanged). `i18n-translation-coverage.test.ts`'s "publishes the honest number" moved to `en: 546/546` with the reason recorded in its note; ru's inherited list (`emailPlaceholder`, `appName`) is unaffected because ru had DECLARED `visibilityShared`, so its removal adds nothing to the inherited set. New guard in `__tests__/visibility-chip-a11y.test.ts` — one case per locale (6) asserting no map re-declares `visibilityShared` — so a future edit reviving a contradictory duplicate of `visibilityViewer` by reflex turns a named case red instead of shipping. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 14/14, 5955 tests (+6), build. No DB migration, no creds.

## The locked chip announced `disabled` and was not (2026-08-20)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Sheet-sandwich follow-ups": the locked private-visibility chip declared `accessibilityState={{ disabled: locked }}` and did not disable — pressing it opens the premium upsell (create screen) or fires a toast (edit modal). A screen reader offering the button as "dimmed, private" gave no cue for the dim and none that the upsell would appear on press. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-20] [est: 8000] **Stop lying about `disabled` on the locked visibility chip, and let it name the premium constraint out loud** — two adoption points, one shape: `app/create-collection.tsx` (chip opens `<PremiumUpsellSheet>`) and `components/edit-collection-modal.tsx` (chip fires a toast). Both chips now declare `accessibilityState={{ selected }}` and NO `disabled` — the button is live, and the state that used to sit there was the honest description of how it LOOKS and a lie about what it DOES. The `accessibilityLabel` is set only on the locked variant, composed from `t("visibilityPrivate")` and `t("visibilityPrivatePremiumOnly")` so a non-visual user hears "Private — Private collections are a premium feature." instead of "Private". Unlocked chips carry no `accessibilityLabel`, so the `<Text>` child's name still composes on iOS. The label is a template literal with two `t()` calls, which the untranslated-label rule in `check-a11y-jsx` (matching bare `"..."` only) ignores by construction, so the fix does not need a new key. Related type narrowing found while writing it: inside the ternary, `v === "public"` is unreachable when `locked` is truthy (because `locked = v === "private" && !isPremium`), so both labels hardcode `t("visibilityPrivate")` — the private-only side of the pair — rather than branching on `v` a second time. New `__tests__/visibility-chip-a11y.test.ts` (6 cases) pins both chips separately — the shape of `accessibilityState`, the absence of any `disabled` key inside it, and both halves of the composed label — because a fix that reached only one screen would leave the other silent. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 14/14, 5944 tests (+6), build. No DB migration, no creds.

- [DONE: 2026-08-20] [est: 7000] **Translate the visibility chip's visible text and hints so the eye and the ear agree** — the composed spoken label went per-locale in the entry above, and the chip's own text (`visibilityPrivate` / `visibilityPublic`) plus the two hints below it (`visibilityPublicHint` / `visibilityPrivateHint`) still fell through `...en` in four of six locales, so a Polish user saw "Private" on the chip and heard the Polish composed sentence via the label — the visible name and the spoken one in different languages. Four keys times four locales (be/pl/de/es — ru already had them), sixteen strings, no code changes. Verified by extending `__tests__/visibility-chip-a11y.test.ts` with a per-key case that goes through `assertMatchesInEveryLocaleBody` — the four keys are pinned as declared per locale, so a locale losing one turns a named case red rather than reducing a translation-coverage floor by one alongside every other churn. The base count went from 547 to 547 (no new keys, just new declarations of existing ones), and ru's inherited list stays at two — brand + email. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 14/14, 5949 tests (+4 new per-key cases), build. No DB migration, no creds.

- [DONE: 2026-08-20] [est: 9000] **Say the locked chip's premium sentence in every language, as ONE composed key rather than two joined at runtime** — the label above shipped as `${t("visibilityPrivate")} — ${t("visibilityPrivatePremiumOnly")}`, and only English declared the second half; five locales fell through the `...en` spread, so a Russian screen reader user hearing the chip got "Private — Private collections are a premium feature." in English inside a Russian UI. Two of the five (ru, be) also lacked `visibilityPrivate`, so the composed sentence would have been English on both halves. Both issues collapse into one key per locale, `visibilityPrivateLockedA11y`, that names the value and the premium constraint together — so each language answers for its own apposition punctuation (a colon rather than an em-dash in ru/be) and the run-time concatenation the guard cannot see through is gone. Six declarations of `visibilityPrivateLockedA11y` and five new declarations of `visibilityPrivatePremiumOnly` (in ru/be/pl/de/es), taking the base count from 546 to 547 — the two consumers now read one key each, `accessibilityLabel={locked ? t("visibilityPrivateLockedA11y") : undefined}`, on both chips. Related brittle-suite finding on the first pass, and fixed rather than routed around: the new suite counted `readI18nSource().match(/visibilityPrivateLockedA11y:/g).length === 6`, which is exactly the shape `i18n-locales-helper.test.ts`'s "the habit stays retired" case forbids — a whole-file count would pass on a base map declaring the key twice while a non-base map inherited the English string. It now goes through `assertMatchesInEveryLocaleBody`, which asks each locale on its own. `translation-coverage`'s "names the three keys Russian still inherits" (down to two now, both legitimate — a brand and an email address) and the "publishes the honest number" report (546 → 547) both moved along with the change. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 14/14, 5945 tests (+1 new case), build. No DB migration, no creds.

## The tappable that never says it is a button (2026-08-20)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Disabled-state follow-ups", and decomposed on arrival because it is four times the size of the icon sweep. Neither React Native's `<Pressable>` nor react-native-web's sets a default role (checked in both sources), so a Pressable with no `accessibilityRole` announces as a generic element on all three platforms: a screen reader user is told there is some text there, not that it can be pressed. 152 of this tree's 194 interactive Pressables are in that state; 42 already carry a role.

Not a codemod, unlike the disabled sweep: `button` is right for most and wrong for several. `imagebutton`, `checkbox`, `link` and `search` are each already correct somewhere in this tree, full-screen backdrop Pressables (`onPress={close}` behind a modal) arguably want no role and no focus at all, and `swipe-tabs` wants the tab treatment the nav bar settled on (`button` + `accessibilityState={{ selected }}`, because React Native has no `tab` role in the subset this app types against).

Split by ownership rather than by count, shared code first: a role added to a component is a role added to every screen that renders it. Each sub-task ships independently and the counts are what the sweep measured on 2026-08-20. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-20] [est: 16000] **The shared components (26)** — a role added here is a role added to every screen that renders them, which is why this slice went first. Most are `button`; the interesting ones are the four that are not. `selectable-item-row` is a `checkbox` with `accessibilityState={{ checked: selected }}`: it is a row in a multi-select list, and "button" would announce the tap while saying nothing about whether the item is in the selection, which is the whole state the row exists to show. `swipe-tabs`' two rows are `button` + `{{ selected: isActive }}`, because `tab` is not in the `AccessibilityRole` union this app types against — the same answer `<NavTab>` settled on, so the app now has one story for tabs rather than two. Three chips that render an "active" style and said nothing about it (a currency chip, a quick filter chip, a reaction chip) now carry `{{ selected }}`. And four Pressables got `accessibilityRole="none"` rather than a role: the backdrop behind the filter sheet and the create sheet, and the wrapper inside each whose only job is `e.stopPropagation()`. `button` on a backdrop puts a full-screen focus stop in front of the sheet, and on the wrapper it announces the sheet itself as pressable — `none` is that decision written down, which an unset role is not. 152 → 126. The new `__tests__/pressable-roles.test.ts` counts ALL interactive Pressables per file rather than the ones this slice touched: five of `item-filters`' ten already carried a role, and a case counting only the five would go green on a file that lost the other five. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 14/14, 5947 tests (+13), build.

- [DONE: 2026-08-20] [est: 16000] **The modals and sheets (32)** — and the group turned out to have exactly one shape repeated seven times. Every sheet in this tree is the same two-Pressable sandwich: a full-screen backdrop whose press dismisses, wrapping a card whose only `onPress` is `e.stopPropagation()` to stop the tap reaching it. Neither is a control — `button` on the backdrop puts a focus stop the size of the screen in front of the sheet, and on the wrapper it announces the sheet itself as pressable — so both take `accessibilityRole="none"`, which is that decision written down rather than the absence of one. That regularity is now its own case: all nine sheets in the tree (the seven here plus the filter sheet and the create sheet from the previous slice) must opt out EXACTLY twice, so a sheet with one is a sheet whose backdrop or wrapper has quietly become a control. The rest are `button`, plus four chips that draw a "chosen" style and said nothing about it — the search overlay's type filter and its two owner chips, and the edit modal's visibility chip, which draws TWO states (`selected` and premium-`locked`) and announced neither: a screen reader user pressing the locked one got a toast and no warning beforehand. 126 → 94. Two codemod artefacts fixed in the same pass rather than left: the role insertion put the new prop mid-line on single-line tags (reflowed, one prop per line) and left a whitespace-only line on tags that were already multi-line (stripped) — this repo has no prettier, so nothing else would have. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 14/14, 5956 tests (+9), build.

- [DONE: 2026-08-20] [est: 15000] **The two big detail screens (32)** — the most per-file and, in the end, the least varied. `app/profile/[id].tsx`'s sixteen are all plain buttons: it is one screen of relationship actions (add friend, remove friend, follow, unfollow, message, admin delete) written out once per `ProfileRelationship`, so there is nothing to decide beyond the role. `app/item/[id].tsx` carries the sheet sandwich again — its listing sheet is the tenth backdrop-plus-`stopPropagation` pair in the tree and takes the same `accessibilityRole="none"` — and two chip rows that draw `conditionChipSelected` and said nothing about it: the condition picker in the edit form and the buy/trade mode picker in the listing sheet. Both share a style AND a local `selected`, which is why one assertion covers both and why a case matching the STYLE would have passed on either of them alone. 94 → 62. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 14/14, 5959 tests (+3), build.

- [DONE: 2026-08-20] [est: 20000] **The remaining 62 screens, and the rule the tree earned** — the list-and-create screens (`people`, `create`, `collection`, `wishlist`) and everything else (`friends`, `_layout`, `create-collection`, `settings`, `chat`, `listing`, `chats`, `index`, `auth/callback`) shipped as one push because the payoff is entirely at zero. `people`'s ten are eight relationship actions across the same `ProfileRelationship` branches `profile/[id]` already covered plus two pagination buttons; `wishlist` and `create` each carry another sheet sandwich; the rest are plain buttons and one more `conditionChip` (in `create.tsx`). Every interactive `<Pressable>` in `app/` and `components/` now announces what it is, 194 of 194, and `no_role` is `check-a11y-jsx`'s fifth code — same pattern as `undecided_icon` and `silent_disabled`: written the moment the count reaches zero, with the tree as the proof it passes. `HAS_ROLE` accepts `accessibilityRole="none"` because that IS a role and a deliberate one — a sheet backdrop opts out with it — and `IS_INTERACTIVE` reads `onPress` because a Pressable without one is a layout wrapper. The temporary `pressable-roles.test.ts` is deleted rather than extended a fifth time (it was written as a holding pen for exactly this), and every fixture in `check-a11y-jsx.test.ts` that opens an interactive `<Pressable>` was given a role so it stops asserting two rules at once — the same pass the hiding fixtures got when `undecided_icon` shipped. Related bug found in the same pass and fixed with a `precise` edit: my role-insertion codemod left whitespace-only lines on already-multi-line tags, which every previous sweep this week has also paid for; this repo has no prettier, so the sweeps are their own formatting. Verified end to end: removing one role from `app/settings.tsx` exits 1 naming the file, the line and what to add, and the tree goes green when it is put back. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 14/14, 5938 tests (5 down from 5959 — the deleted suite), build.


## Greyed out and saying nothing (2026-08-20)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, "Sweep-complete follow-ups": the disabled state was found on one button while deciding the wishlist's icons, and the entry said others almost certainly had it. Swept properly this time before touching anything, which is the lesson the icon sweep's own follow-ups kept repeating. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-20] [est: 15000] **Say when a button is disabled, in all 29 places that did not** — greyed-out is a colour, and a colour reaches nobody: without `accessibilityState={{ disabled }}` a screen reader offers the button as though pressing it would do something. 29 of this tree's 33 disabled `<Pressable>`s were silent, including both of the photo lightbox's chevrons at the first and last photo, every disabled button on the login screen, and both bulk-bar actions. Unlike the icon sweep this needed no judgement at all — a `<Pressable>` that takes `disabled` should say so, with no per-site decision — so it went in as one codemod over `app/` and `components/`, brace-depth aware and string-literal aware so an expression containing `>` could not end an open tag early, inserting the state next to the `disabled` prop it mirrors. The rule then went into `check-a11y-jsx` the same way `undecided_icon` did an hour earlier: written the moment the count reached zero, with the tree as the proof it passes. Two details it is easy to get wrong and both are pinned — `accessibilityState` has to NAME disabled rather than merely exist (a tab carrying `{{ selected }}` beside a `disabled` prop is exactly as silent as one carrying nothing, and this tree has such tabs), and the rule is scoped to `<Pressable>` because `<DangerSection disabled>` forwards it to a Pressable that announces it correctly — a rule that could not see inside the component would be wrong about both of its call sites. Verified end to end by deleting one of the 29 back out: the guard exits 1 naming `components/bulk-bar.tsx:41` and what to add, and goes green when it is put back. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 14/14, 5934 tests (+7), build. No DB migration, no creds.

## The rule the tree had to earn (2026-08-20)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md, top entry of "Sweep-complete follow-ups" — filed by the run that finished the sweep an hour earlier, which is what made this writable at all. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-20] [est: 17000] **Make "every icon is hidden or named" a guard, now that the count is zero** — `check-a11y-icon-labels` reported a node hidden on SOME platforms and said nothing about one hidden on NONE, so the whole day's sweep was held by per-file counts in one suite: they catch a new icon in the ten files they name and not in a new file. `undecided_icon` closes that. It is a rule that could not have been written this morning for a reason worth recording — with 36 findings it gets floored at 36 and stops meaning anything; the day the count reaches zero it becomes a rule with the tree as its proof. Hiding is INHERITED, so the walk keeps a stack of open tags rather than scanning them flat: `item-card.tsx`'s photo-count badge is a glyph AND a bare number hidden together as a subtree, and a flat rule would report both icons and demand redundant props on them. That stack also closes a suggestion filed this morning against `half_hidden` — a child inside an already-hidden parent is no longer reported as partially hidden. Android is what makes the ancestry more than a detail and it has its own table: `importantForAccessibility="no"` hides the node and leaves its children announced, and only `"no-hide-descendants"` takes the subtree, so `HIDES_SUBTREE_BY` differs from `HIDDEN_BY` on exactly that one platform — pinned by a case, because the two tables looking almost identical is how they end up merged by someone tidying. The rule deliberately does NOT accept a label on an ancestor `<Pressable>` as the icon's decision: on iOS that label REPLACES the subtree, so the icon is silent there and announced on the web, which is the split this guard exists to catch. Verified end to end rather than by fixture alone: a planted `<Ionicons name="planted-offender" />` in `components/empty-state.tsx` exits 1 naming the file, the line and what to do, and the tree goes green again when it is removed. The guard's own suite grew a `HIDDEN` fixture const and moved its hiding cases onto `<Text>`, because a fixture built from a bare `<Ionicons>` now asserts two rules at once and stops saying which one it is for. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 14/14, 5927 tests (+7), build. No DB migration, no creds.

## The badge a screen reader could not hear (2026-08-20)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md: the top entry of "Icon-label follow-ups" (2026-08-20). That entry named the cost as "a plural-aware string in six languages" and pointed at `photosCount` to copy; the copy turned out to be the answer to a different question — see below. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-20] [est: 18000] **Say the nav badges out loud, since they are drawn inside the label that silences them** — the chats tab draws an unread count and the friends tab a pending-request dot, both inside the `<Pressable>` that carries the tab's `accessibilityLabel`. A screen reader announces the label and stops, so the bar said "Chats, button" at zero unread and at forty: the one thing each badge exists to convey was the one thing a non-visual user never got. Two shapes of badge, two shapes of label — the count badge shows a number so its label says the number, and the friends badge is a dot that shows only THAT something is waiting, so its label says that and no more. Announcing a count the screen does not show would be its own mismatch, in the other direction. The label is derived from the BADGE rather than from the counter behind it: `navTabLabelSpec` in `lib/chat-helpers.ts` maps `FriendsTabBadge` onto `plain | unread | pending`, exhaustively, so a fourth badge kind has to answer "and what does this one say out loud?" before it compiles — and reading `unreadTotal` a second time in `bottom-nav.tsx` would have been a second place for the pill and the spoken name to disagree. It also catches a case the counter would not: `formatBadgeCount(0)` is `""`, so a zero-valued count badge draws nothing, and a label reading "unread: 0" beside no pill is exactly the mismatch this routing exists to prevent. The label deliberately does NOT use `formatBadgeCount`: its "99+" cap exists because the pill is 18px wide, and a spoken label has no width, so 240 unread is announced as 240. On the plural problem the suggestion flagged: the fix was to not have it. `Chats, unread: ${count}` and its five translations put the number after a colon, which is a form Russian, Belarusian and Polish read correctly at 1, 3 and 5 — copying `photosCount`'s `${count} фото` would have needed three-way agreement in three of the six locales and gets it wrong at 2. Both keys are written in all six languages rather than inherited from `en`, which took the total to 545. Related bug found and fixed while writing the suite, and it is the repo's own guard finding it: the first draft of `__tests__/nav-badge-a11y.test.ts` asserted `bodies.length === 6` over the whole translations file, which `i18n-locales-helper.test.ts` retires repo-wide for a good reason — six hits somewhere in the file is satisfied by ONE locale declaring the key six times. It is now one pattern per locale body that pins the formatter shape and the `${params?.count ?? 0}` interpolation together, so a locale whose label is a constant sentence fails on that locale's name. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 14/14, 5894 tests (+13), build. No DB migration, no creds.

## The two props that cover two of this app's three platforms (2026-08-20)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md: the top entry of "Paired-prop follow-ups" (2026-08-20), filed by the run that shipped the pairing rule an hour earlier. That entry called this "a bigger hole than the one just closed" and it was right: the pair it had just pinned everywhere covers iOS and Android, and this app's primary surface is the browser. The entry left the approach open — a `Platform.OS === "web"` branch or a prop only react-native-web reads — and the answer turned out to be neither. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-20] [est: 16000] **Hide the decorative icons from the browser too, which the paired rule never asked them to** — `accessibilityElementsHidden` is iOS and `importantForAccessibility="no"` is Android, and react-native-web reads neither. All seven decorative `<Ionicons>` in this tree carried both, passed the guard that shipped an hour earlier, and were announced by every screen reader on the web — the surface this app ships to first. The fix is `aria-hidden`, which is one prop rather than the `Platform.OS === "web"` branch the suggestion expected: react-native-web forwards it to the DOM attribute of the same name. It is an ADDITION to the native pair and not a replacement for it, which is the part worth writing down: React Native's `<View>` derives `accessibilityElementsHidden` + `importantForAccessibility="no-hide-descendants"` from `aria-hidden` (`View.js` does this explicitly), so on a `<View>` the one prop would have been enough — and `<Text>` does not, and `<Ionicons>` renders a `<Text>`. Every one of the seven sites is an icon, so dropping the native pair for the single prop would have silently un-hidden all of them on iOS and Android. The guard is now three-way rather than paired: `HIDE_PLATFORMS` is `["ios", "android", "web"]`, `HIDDEN_BY` maps each to the source shape that HIDES (still matching on the instruction and not the prop name — `importantForAccessibility="yes"` is the opposite instruction, and `aria-hidden={false}` joins `accessibilityElementsHidden={false}` as not-a-hide, with the `{ false }` spelling now accepted too), and a finding carries `missing: HidePlatform[]` so the report says "still announced on web — add aria-hidden (web)" rather than restating the rule. The web pattern needs a lookbehind rather than `\b`: `-` is already a word boundary, so `data-aria-hidden` would have satisfied a `\b` version, and there is a case for that. Both ends stay non-findings by construction — hidden everywhere is done, hidden nowhere never asked to be — which is what stops the rule turning every plain `<View>` into a finding, and is now pinned rather than implied. The two count assertions in `sheet-search-row.test.ts` (2 icons here, 3 in `search-overlay.tsx`) count the web prop alongside the other two, with the same lookbehind. Verified end to end rather than by source match: `aria-hidden` appears 17 times in the exported web bundle, so the prop survives Metro and the `<Text>` spread in `@expo/vector-icons`' `create-icon-set`, which is the step a source assertion cannot see. `npm run verify` green: typecheck, `lint:all` 14/14, 5881 tests (+8), build. No DB migration, no creds.

The settings picker listed all six in the same chip, the same weight and the same order — a promise of parity the app cannot keep, and a reader who picked one of the four met a mostly-English interface with no way to know that was expected. Badged rather than hidden or reordered: hiding takes a language from the reader who wants it half-translated and knows it, and sorting by coverage rewrites a familiar list every time a translation lands; a badge changes what the picker CLAIMS without changing what it offers. `lib/translation-status.ts` is the verdict the picker can import — `lib/i18n-coverage.ts` PARSES the source of `lib/i18n-context.tsx`, which is a thing a test does and an app cannot. It commits the CLASSIFICATION and not the percentages, for the reason `TRANSLATION_FLOORS` gives next door: "de is at 38.8%" goes red on every feature PR that adds an English string, because the denominator moves and every row falls, while "de is one of the partial ones" is stable under exactly that churn and still catches both events worth catching — a locale finished and still badged, and one that collapsed into the set with nothing said. `__tests__/translation-status.test.ts` derives the set from the real measurement and asserts equality (premise first: fewer than six rows and the comparison proves nothing), pins that the base language and `ru` stay unqualified, that every badged code is one the picker actually offers, that the two groups are ≥20 points apart so the 90% threshold separates two genuinely different things, and that an unmeasured code answers false rather than throwing inside a render. The badge sits UNDER the label rather than beside it — `Беларуская` plus a badge on one line pushes the wrapped row to three lines on a narrow phone — and the qualification goes on the Pressable's `accessibilityLabel`, because a badge announced after the language name is one a reader meets after they have already chosen. Two new keys, translated into all six locales rather than left to inherit, which is why every coverage row ROSE by two. Two unrelated fixes on the way: `check-inline-hex`'s floor was drawn level with `lib/`'s own count by the new module (160 = 160, so a walk that lost both `app/` and `components/` would pass) and is re-measured to 168 of 224 — the third time for that reason and the second time by `lib/`; and `__tests__/lint-guard-invalid-floor.test.ts` hardcoded `minimum: 160` in the rewrite it patches the table with, so on the run that raised the floor the mutation silently matched nothing, the "broken" copy stayed valid and two cases failed claiming the guard would not refuse — it reads the number from `SCANNED_FLOORS` now, since a fixture that hardcodes the value it mutates breaks on exactly the maintenance it exists to survive. Mutation-checked two ways: dropping `es` from the list turns the equality and the gap cases, adding `ru` turns those two plus the unqualified-locale case. 7 files, 5 new cases. `npm run verify` green: 5786 tests, 1310 suites, 13/13 guards, build exported. No DB migration, no creds.

- [DONE: 2026-08-20] [est: 11000] **Say why the bundle scan walks, pin it against git, and run the two-oddity checkout** — `check-secrets` takes its candidates from `git ls-files` and NAMES the set in its report; `check-bundle-secrets` walks and said nothing about what it looked at, so a reader meeting the two side by side could not tell "not applicable" from "not thought about". Five suggestions in five sections asked for the same sentence. It is written now, and it is not a fallback story: `.gitignore` covers `dist/` whole, so git's listing names NOTHING under it and asking would hand the scan an empty candidate set over a directory that exists and is full — and the set this guard reports on is not what git would take but what the browser was SENT, which is exactly what is on disk. Three properties ride on that walk and each is free only because it walks: the regular-file question (`entry.isFile()` on every entry, which is the hole `partitionRegularFiles` exists to close on the git path), the unread clause (a walk only ever names what it just met), and the `.local.` convention (nothing generates such a name into `dist/`, and a build that emitted one would have SHIPPED it, so honouring the rule here would skip the one copy that matters). Prose nothing can break is how the `NEVER_WALKED` drift started, so `__tests__/bundle-walk-premise.test.ts` turns each half red on a change: git's listing holds no path under `dist/` (asserting its own premise first — git must have listed something under `scripts/`, or the case passes over a listing that came back empty for its own reasons), `.gitignore` still covers `dist`, `BUNDLE_SKIP_DIRS` is still empty against a source list that is not, and the guard's code — through `stripComments`, because the header EXPLAINS the omission and a raw match reads that explanation as the thing it forbids — still does not reach for `isLocalOnlyPath`. Second half is the sentence a reader actually meets: the local-only skip clause and the unread clause are appended by one `map`, each proven alone and never together, so one fixture now plants BOTH (an ignore file that deliberately does not cover `*.local.*`, since the repository's own would drop the file before the scan saw it) and asserts the one line, both commas, git's verdict still on the skip clause, and the clauses following the subject they qualify. And the precedence the two single-reason cases cannot show: a checkout holding an unread symlink AND a committed file the working tree lacks must FAIL, name the missing file, and leave the link out of a refusal that tells its reader to restore things — a link that is exactly where it should be is the wrong instruction. Mutation-checked two ways, each turning exactly two cases red and leaving the other pair green: dropping the unread clause turns the two clause cases, making every unread kind benign turns the two refusal cases. 3 files, 7 new cases. `npm run verify` green: 5781 tests, 1309 suites, 13/13 guards, build exported. No DB migration, no creds.

Both callers ask for `-z` now and both go through one `namesFrom`, which splits on NUL, drops the trailing empty field and deduplicates; the three paragraphs that were spread across two functions (why `-z`, why the dedup, why no trimming) are written once beside it, and `listCommittable` keeps its `.sort()` at the call site because it is the only one of the two a report counts through. Three cases at the unit level and one at the run level, and the run case is what proves the product: two force-added `.local.` copies, one of them accented, asserting the refusal counts `2 file(s)`, prints both names as they are spelled on disk, sorts them, and contains no `\303` anywhere — which also gives the PLURAL path of that formatter its first spawn, having been exercised only by unit cases over the string. Each case asserts its own premise before its claim, so a checkout with `core.quotePath=false` or a git that stopped quoting fails loudly instead of passing over a bug it never met. Mutation-checked two ways, each turning exactly the expected cases red: restoring the exact pre-change reader (line split, trim, no `-z`) turns the three unit cases and the run case, and printing the escaped spelling in the report; adding the trim back INSIDE the shared reader turns only the two that are about trimming, which is what separates the two defects. 4 files, 4 new cases. `npm run verify` green: 5765 tests, 1306 suites, 13/13 guards, build exported. The `.local.` rule closed the hole in one direction; this closes the noise in the other, and the noise has a cost: a scratch `notes.md` with a pasted key, an editor backup, a half-finished migration are all read and all reported at the severity of a committed credential, and a guard that fails over files nobody can commit is one people learn to run with their eyes closed. The fixture question that blocked this is answered by keeping the walk: every guard fixture is a temp directory with no `.git` above it, so the fallback is the normal path for the harness rather than an exotic one, and the pass line NAMES which of the two it read (`from git's committable set` / `from the working tree (not checked against git: …)`) — two scans reporting identically over different sets is the failure this whole line of work is about. Both candidate sources go through one `pick`, and `selectPaths` in `scripts/guard-io.ts` applies the walk's own two filters (case-folded extension, skip-list by path SEGMENT) to a list nobody walked, so the two cannot drift on which candidates they take. Proved by a run inside a work tree with an ignored offender (passes), one with an untracked offender (fails), one outside git at all (walks, and says so), and a case asserting the git listing loses no tracked file the walk finds.

So each of the four cheap guards is now run over a scratch tree with a PLANTED offender, and the path it prints is joined back onto the root it was pointed at and opened — present, readable, and not absolute, since a report that names `/home/runner/work/collectables/collectables/app/…` is unreadable without knowing where CI checked out. The offenders are the smallest thing each matcher rejects, written out rather than borrowed, because an offender that already exists would let the case pass on a walk that ignored the plant. Two details the suite had to get right or it would have passed for the wrong reason: the reported path is matched by the PLANT's own basename rather than "the first path-shaped thing in the output", since `check-inline-hex`'s report tells the reader to route the value through `lib/design-tokens.ts` — a real file, present in the fixture, resolving happily and saying nothing about the finding; and the three guards print the location three ways (bare, `:line`, `:line:column`), so the line and column are deliberately not part of the match, because what is under test is the BASE. Mutation-checked rather than assumed: repointing `check-inline-hex` at an absolute path turns exactly one case red and leaves the other seven green. `check-problem-phrasing-imports` is asserted at the source instead, with the reason stated — its floor of 450 spans five roots including `__tests__/`, so a fixture that gets far enough to print a report has to copy four hundred files per run to prove three lines the other four already prove — and the coverage case requires every `listSourceFiles` caller to be in one list or the other, so a new walking guard cannot arrive without one. `check-bundle-secrets` gets the same treatment for the same reason (the bundle premise refuses without a real `dist/`), and `check-secrets` gets it for a sharper one: its `SKIP_FILES` list is written in repo-relative paths, so a walk whose base moved would silently stop skipping the scanner's own sources and start reporting them. 1 file, 8 cases. `npm run verify` green: 5683 tests, 1293 suites, 13/13 guards, build exported. No DB migration, no creds.

No DB migration, no creds.

## The value the pattern was allowed to borrow from the next key (2026-08-18)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md: the second entry of "Per-locale-assertion follow-ups", which named the remaining hole in the assertions migrated earlier today and said the fix was a value span the scanner could already reach. - [DONE: 2026-08-18] [est: 20000] **Teach the scanner to return each key's VALUE, and match value claims against it instead of against the map** — the per-locale assertions migrated this morning scoped their patterns to one locale's body, which closed the cross-map hole and left the same mistake one level down: `key:[\s\S]*?SHAPE` starts at its own key and strides through however many later entries it takes to find the shape, so `loadMoreItems routes count through a ?? 0` was satisfied by ANY later key in that map carrying a `?? 0`. `ParsedObjectLiteral` now carries `values` — a `ReadonlyMap` of each `name:` key to its exact source span, colon to the comma that ends the entry (or to the end of the body, which is the ordinary case for the last one, not the defensive one). Shorthand keys (`{ en, ru }`) are in `keys` and not in `values`: there is no value written at that level and an empty string would be a claim about one. A real scanner bug surfaced the moment spans were collected: `(` and `)` were not counted at all, so a comma inside an argument list read as a top-level separator — brace depth alone had been enough while only KEYS were collected (the identifier after such a comma is not followed by a `:`, so nothing was recorded and the mistake cost nothing), and with spans it truncated `(p) => String(p.name).replace(/}/g, "")` mid-call. Parens are counted separately from `depth` — the two closers are not interchangeable, and a `}` may not close the literal from inside an open call either, so `atTopLevel()` reads both — saved and restored across template `${}` frames alongside the brace depth, and floored at zero so a stray `)` cannot make every key below it invisible. Three new test helpers on top: `localeValuesOf` (one key's value per locale, throwing on a locale that does not declare it), `assertValueInEveryLocale` (the exact-span counterpart of `assertMatchesInEveryLocale`, whose failure names the locale AND prints the value it rejected), and `localeStrings` (the unquoted copy, refusing a value that is not a plain string literal so a key that became a formatter in one language is a named failure rather than a silently skipped comparison). Nine value-shape call sites migrated across eight suites; `localeValues` — the pattern-and-capture-group form added this morning — is deleted rather than left beside them, since every caller now asks by key. Pinned by 5 new cases in `__tests__/i18n-source.test.ts` (55 in that suite: the span itself with both ends anchored, the argument-list comma, shorthand keys having no value, a value's own braces/parens/regex staying inside it, and a sweep asserting every key of every real locale map has a non-empty span — a key without one would reach callers as "this locale does not declare that key", a translation finding rather than a parser bug) and 5 in `__tests__/i18n-locales-helper.test.ts` (18 there), built on a fixture where `greeting` is the string "Hello" and a later key carries the count shape: the body-wide rule is asserted to PASS on it before the exact rule is shown to fail, so the strengthening is demonstrated rather than claimed. ## The slice that did not slice (2026-08-18)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md: the first entry of "Per-locale-assertion follow-ups", filed earlier today. Every .tasks.md task proper remains blocked — [needs-data] on a 30-day PostHog window, two on dependency decisions. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-18] [est: 15000] **Retire the per-locale slice, which was checking a locale against the whole rest of the file** — eight cases in six suites asserted "ru / be / pl / de / es each override this key rather than falling back to en" with `const ${lang}: TranslationMap = \{[\s\S]*?KEY:[\s\S]*?\};`. It does not slice. `[\s\S]*?` crosses `};` like any other character, so the match ran from the named map's opening brace, through however many LATER maps it took to find the key, and out to the next `};` after that — the key had only to exist somewhere at or below the named locale. For `ru`, the first map in the file, that is the whole file: the assertion was very nearly vacuous, and which locale it could actually speak for was decided by declaration order. Replaced with `assertMatchesInEveryNonBaseLocale`, which tests each map's own body and skips the base (a map cannot fall back to itself). Three more whole-file counters surfaced on the way, and the way they surfaced is the finding: the sweep guard shipped this morning used `/\.(length|size), 6\b/` with a literal space, so `assert.equal(\n  matches.length,\n  6,\n)` — prettier's wrapping of the same expression — went unseen, and the guard reported three suites clean while they were still counting. `collection-detail-sort-gate`, `item-filters-search-ui` and `settings-events-taxonomy` are migrated and the guard now reads `\s*`. Pinned by 3 new cases in `__tests__/i18n-locales-helper.test.ts` (13 in that suite) built on a fixture where `ru` is declared BEFORE `es` and lacks the key `es` carries: the old slice pattern is asserted to MATCH that text — so the hole is demonstrated rather than described — while the new helper names `ru`; plus a case showing the base map is skipped (`en` written as a formatter, the others as strings, so the string pattern is false of `en` and true of every inheriting locale, which is exactly the difference between the two helpers), and the `/g` refusal the sibling helpers carry. ## The parity check that was a sum (2026-08-18)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md: the first entry of "One-path follow-ups", filed on the previous run. Every .tasks.md task proper remains blocked — [needs-data] on a 30-day PostHog window, two on dependency decisions. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-18] [est: 18000] **Ask each locale map whether it declares the key, instead of counting six hits across the file** — twenty-two cases in eleven suites asked "is this key translated everywhere?" by matching `key:` against the whole of `lib/i18n-context.tsx`, counting, and asserting 6. It reads as a parity check and it is a SUM: any arrangement adding to six satisfies it, so a key declared twice in one locale and missing from another is green — which is the exact state the check existed to find — and when it does fail it names no language ("expected 6, got 5"). Three more faults rode along. Several variants matched a bare `key:` ANYWHERE, so a key named inside a template counted as a declaration. Several anchored on `^  key:`, i.e. on prettier's two-space indent, which is the formatting dependency `lib/i18n-source.ts` exists to end. And the six was a literal, so a seventh language would turn all twenty-two red at once with "expected 6, got 7" — reading as the new locale being wrong rather than as twenty-two numbers needing a bump. New `__tests__/helpers/i18n-locales.ts` answers the question through the parser: `locales()` (the picker's codes, so a seventh language is CHECKED rather than counted), `assertDeclaredInEveryLocale` (via `localeKeys`, and an inherited key counts as undeclared — which is the whole distinction, since a key served through `...en` renders, fits the layout and returns a non-empty `t()`), `assertMatchesInEveryLocale` for value-shape claims, and `localeValues` for the "six distinct strings" cases. Two hazards closed in the helpers themselves: both pattern-taking helpers REFUSE a `/g` regex, because a global pattern carries `lastIndex` between locales and would pass for one and fail for the next depending on walk order; and `localeValues` throws on a locale that does not match rather than returning a short map, because five values from six locales are trivially distinct. A real bug found while migrating `item-filters-search-ui.test.ts`: the label-vs-placeholder case zipped two separate whole-file sweeps by INDEX, so one locale missing either key shifted every later pair by one and compared a Polish label against a German placeholder — a pass or a failure decided by declaration order. It pairs by locale code now. Pinned by `__tests__/i18n-locales-helper.test.ts`, 10 cases, built around two fixtures that are GREEN under the old rule and RED under the new one — the sum-but-no-cover arrangement is asserted to satisfy the old count first, so the strengthening is shown rather than claimed — plus the inherited-key case, the mentioned-in-a-value case, the two `/g` refusals, the by-code pairing, and a directory sweep (with `bundle-smoke.test.ts` exempted and the reason written down: its six counts privacy pages, not locales) that keeps the habit retired. Mutation-checked against the real file: deleting `de`'s own `searchClose` makes `sheet-search-row.test.ts` fail with "'searchClose' is not declared in de", where the old count would have needed the total to move. `npm run verify` green: typecheck + lint:all 13/13 + 5563 tests (+10) + build. No DB migration, no creds.

## What stands between the word and the slash, and the number that was six tokens (2026-08-18)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md: the second, third, fourth and fifth entries of "Reserved-word-partition follow-ups". The first entry of that section is deferred with the standing `lib/js-scan.ts` move it depends on, "Keyword-table follow-ups" says outright its one entry is not worth doing for a single caller, and "Member-access follow-ups" is explicitly parked until a third lookbehind rule exists. - [DONE: 2026-08-18] [est: 13000] **Give every keyword row the separator it uses, read a number as one token, and say which productions the contextual list was drawn from** — four findings from the partition audit, and one of them turned out to be a live bug rather than an invariant. (1) The row-shape case had quietly stopped checking the thing that matters. It matched `word` + one space + regex literally until the three ASI rows (`break`, `continue`, `debugger`) arrived, then loosened to "any whitespace" for ALL nineteen rows at once — so a `return` row rewritten across a line break would still have passed, and whether a `/` can stand on the word's own line (i.e. whether there is a division for the scanner to get wrong) was no longer stated anywhere. Rows are `KeywordRegExpRow` now — `{ example, separator }` with `separator: "inline" | "newline"` — and each row is checked against the separator it DECLARES: inline rows must write the regex one space after the word, newline rows a line break after it. Three cases: the per-row match, an assertion that the newline set is exactly the three statement-enders (so a future row cannot reach for `"newline"` to dodge writing a same-line example), and a mutation case that reformats every inline example across a line break and requires the strict pattern to reject it. (2) `consumed()` was documented as one call per token and numbers rode the loop's per-CHARACTER tail — `1_000n` was six calls — which was filed as harmless because only the last character survives. It was not harmless: the tail resynced INSIDE the literal, so a numeric property name like `0x2f:` was consumed as the digit `0` and then `x2f` hit the identifier branch with `expectKey` still true, found the colon a lookahead away, and was pushed as a key. Not a key the scanner declined to read — a key that is in no file at all, invented from the middle of a number and handed to every caller counting keys. Every form carrying a letter or an underscore had it (radix prefixes, exponents, BigInt suffixes, separators); plain `1:` never did, which is why nothing caught it. Numbers now go through `NUMERIC_LITERAL` (anchored `y`, radix prefixes ordered FIRST so `0x1f` is not read as `0` + `x1f`) and `startsNumericLiteral`, which admits a leading `.` only when a digit follows so the member-access dot the identifier branch reads back is untouched. (3) `CONTEXTUAL_KEYWORDS` is eight words chosen by judgement and cannot be made exhaustive the way `RESERVED_WORDS` was — it is not a spec production — so the doc now names the productions that WERE consulted (`ForInOfStatement`, accessor `MethodDefinition` names, import/export clauses, `AsyncFunctionDefinitions`, `ClassElement` modifiers, `LexicalDeclaration`) and the ones deliberately skipped because no `/` can follow the word in them (the strict-mode `FutureReservedWord` modifiers, `accessor`), which makes an omission reviewable instead of invisible. (4) The file now has three load-bearing claims nothing executable can check — the `.` in `DIVISION_FOLLOWS` (its counter-example `p./re/` is a syntax error and cannot be written into a fixture that compiles), the ASI rows, and `skipRegExpLiteral`'s newline bound — and they are named together beside `DIVISION_FOLLOWS` so the next reader reads their untestability as "this is a claim about JavaScript" rather than as an excuse to delete them. Pinned by 8 new cases in `__tests__/i18n-source.test.ts` (50 in that suite): the three separator cases above, a sweep dividing after hex/octal/binary/BigInt/exponent/signed-exponent/separator/leading-dot literals in one fixture, a case proving the leading-dot gate does not disturb `p.return / 2` or `...p`, and the phantom-key case, which is red against the old scanner and green against the new one. `npm run verify` green: typecheck + lint:all 13/13 + 5548 tests (+8) + build. No DB migration, no creds.

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md: the second and third entries of "Keyword-table follow-ups" (the `Set` derived at import with nothing pinning the agreement, and the hand-enumerated exclusion lists) and the first entry of "Member-access follow-ups" (the decimal point the member-dot clause was said to be accidentally right about). - [DONE: 2026-08-17] [est: 7000] **Make the keyword partition exhaustive against ECMA-262, drop the set derived from it, and divide after a trailing-dot number** — three findings, two of them from auditing the suggestion rather than from taking it at its word. (1) `REGEXP_KEYWORDS` was a `Set` rebuilt from the table's keys at import — two structures that could not drift the day they were written, which is exactly the pair a later hand-edit drifts: the membership cases read the RECORD and the scanner read the SET, so a hand-written set would have changed what the scanner did with every case green. There is one structure now (`Object.hasOwn` on the table), so there is nothing left to agree, and `hasOwn` rather than `in` is itself a fix — `constructor` and `toString` are `in` any object literal, so a value named `constructor` followed by a `/` would have opened a regex. (2) The two exclusion lists were hand-typed, which is the recall problem the table escaped one level up: a word in NEITHER the table nor an exclusion is invisible — no case names it, so nothing says which way a `/` after it goes. Both sides are derived from one authority now, ECMA-262 §12.7.2's `ReservedWord` production (38 words, pinned by count and by content so the partition cannot be made exhaustive by deleting the word that broke it) plus the eight contextual keywords that can stand before a `/` — `of` is a table row and is not reserved, which is why the spec's list alone is not enough. The partition is asserted exhaustive AND disjoint, and each of the 27 exclusions carries its reason ("takes a `(`", "ends an expression") rather than "is not a keyword", which is not a reason when half of them are keywords. Running the audit added three rows: `break`, `continue` and `debugger` end a statement, so ASI closes it at the line break and the `/re/` below opens a new one — a regex CAN follow them, no valid program divides there, so a row costs nothing and their absence was the invisible case the exhaustiveness check exists to catch. (3) The suggestion said the member-dot clause was accidentally right about `1.5 / 2` too. It never ran for it — a digit is not an identifier start, so `prev === "."` is unreachable from a number and `1.5` divided because it ends in a DIGIT. Which left the shape the coincidence was hiding: `2.` is a legal numeric literal, `.` was outside `DIVISION_FOLLOWS`, and `2. / 2` opened a regex that swallowed the `}` closing its `${}` slot, the template, the `,` and every key below — the keyword hole again, reached through a number instead of a word. A `.` cannot precede a `/` in any other valid program (`p./re/` is a syntax error, no statement begins with a dot), so the class gets it unconditionally. 5 new cases (the trailing-dot and decimal divisions, the inherited-property-name division, the exhaustive/disjoint partition, and the reserved-word list pinned), replacing the two hand-enumerated exclusion cases with one derived from the record. Mutation-checked three ways, each turning exactly one case red: removing the `.` from the class, spelling membership `in` instead of `Object.hasOwn`, and dropping the `break` row. `npm run verify` green: typecheck + lint:all 13/13 + 5543 tests (+3 net) + build. No DB migration, no creds.

Two observations, one syscall apart, with no memory between them: they can disagree, and the shape that makes them disagree is not exotic — a slow `npm ci` running in another terminal lands an install between the walk and the sentence. The refusal then describes a state that had already stopped being true when the decision was made, which is the one thing a diagnosis cannot afford, because a reader who checks the claim and finds it false stops believing the paragraph that names their real problem. `ChainProvenance` now carries a `ProbedLink` for the nearest link exactly as the walk saw it, and `nearestEmptyLink(root)` splits into `emptyLinkFrom(nearest, readdir)` — pure but for one listing, taking the walk's answer — and `probeNearestLink(root)`, kept for the ONE refusal that has no walk behind it: when `require.resolve` itself throws there is no provenance to carry anything out of, so that message still has to ask. The `readdir` seam covers the branch that had none: a listing that FAILS is not this probe's finding, because the link is then unreadable and that has its own path, its own sentence and its own list, and answering here too would send the reader to fix one directory for two different reasons. 4 new cases (no chain at all, a link the walk could not resolve, the realpath read while the reader's own path is reported — the split that matters for a symlinked install — and the failed listing declining to speak). Mutation-checked both ways: reading the asked-about path instead of the resolved one turns the symlink row red, and reporting the failed listing as an emptiness finding turns the declining row red. `npm run verify` green: typecheck + lint:all 13/13 + 5435 tests (+4) + build. No DB migration, no creds.

What made that affordable is the other half: the clause builders were pure functions reachable only THROUGH a refusal, so every row of them cost a planted directory and a spawned child, which is why "no findings in, no clause out" — a one-row table — was being proved with two `NODE_PATH` plants and a symlink cycle. `obstructionClause` and `unreadableList` are exported as they were; `emptyLinkClause` took the bigger change, from `(root, host)` to `(EmptyLink | null, host)`, moving the `nearestEmptyLink` probe out to the two call sites so the renderer is pure and the filesystem stays with the half that needs one. The spawns stay exactly where their claim needs them — that a clause reaches a message a REAL walk produced — and nowhere else. 6 new cases (the empty clause for each of the three builders, the singular/plural agreement across four slots in one sentence with both paths listed, the path named exactly once per host, and the separator that has to stay a separator), plus `renderedIn` wired into all four tables. ## The errno blamed on the call that succeeded (2026-08-16)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md: the second, third and fourth entries of "Obstruction-table follow-ups" — the structurally-typed `lstat` seam, the `statSync` failure caught by the `realpath` handler, and the `UnreadableLink.detail` that renders identically for two different directories. All three are the same report pointing at the wrong place, so they ship together. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-16] [est: 8000] **Name the syscall on an unreadable link, split the `stat` out of the `realpath` catch, and make the injected `lstat` a real one** — the unreadable-link report prints `<path> (<errno>)` and the path it prints is the chain link, whichever call produced the errno, which made it wrong in two cases out of three. An `EACCES` from `realpath` means the link cannot be traversed and the reader should look at the link; the SAME code from `lstat` means its PARENT could not be read, so the link itself may be perfectly fine and the `chmod` they were sent to run changes nothing. `UnreadableLink` now carries a `LinkSyscall`, the wording of what each one implicates lives in an exported `SYSCALL_IMPLICATION` under the phrase rulebook shipped an hour earlier, and the sentence reads `<path> (<detail> on <syscall> — <implication>)`. The second half is the misattribution that made the third row possible: `realpathSync` and `statSync` sat in one `try`, so a `stat` that failed landed in the handler written to classify the FIRST call — a target removed between the two, or a filesystem that answers `realpath` and then refuses `stat`, produced an errno attributed to a syscall that had already succeeded. The window is a race and the message is its only artifact, so `probeResolvedLink(real, stat = fs.statSync)` is split out with its own catch and its own syscall tag; a `stat` reporting plain ABSENCE gets no finding at all, because the path is genuinely gone as of the second observation and that is what every ancestor of every checkout looks like. Third, the injected `lstat` was typed `{ isSymbolicLink(): boolean }` — satisfied by the real syscall AND by a two-line stub, which is the seam working, but also by nothing else the probe might grow: an `.isFile()` or `.mode` check added later compiles against `fs` and throws `is not a function` on every injected row at once. It asks for `fs.Stats` now, and the rows supply real ones taken from a planted symlink and a planted file, which is a more honest fixture than an object that answers one question. 6 new cases (the stat race in three errnos, the vanished target in two, the default stat against a real directory and a real file, plus the table's three rows and the assertion that its rows send the reader to three different places). Mutation-checked both ways: dropping the syscall from the rendered message turns 2 cases red, and re-attributing the `stat` failure to `realpath` turns the race rows red. `npm run verify` green: typecheck + lint:all 13/13 + 5425 tests (+6) + build. No DB migration, no creds.

## The rule with one home, the tables with one rulebook, and the tail that was only true once (2026-08-16)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md: the second, third and fifth entries of "Package-counting follow-ups", filed by the run directly below — the undotted-name rule enforced inline, the two phrasing tables disagreeing about periods for a reason recorded nowhere, and the clause tail carried into a refusal it is false about. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-16] [est: 9000] **Name the package-entry rule, put the phrase tables under one rulebook, and split the clause tail per refusal** — three findings about the same clause, all of the form "a decision was made and not written down". (1) The rule the emptiness probe turns on — a `node_modules` entry is loadable iff it is UNDOTTED — was a doc comment over an inline `!entry.startsWith(".")` at one call site: a claim about how node resolves, stated in prose and enforced in an expression, with no way to read the two against each other. (2) `OBSTRUCTION_PHRASE` forbade a period anywhere and `EMPTY_LINK_PHRASE` forbade one only at the end, which is a real difference for a real reason — the second legitimately names `.package-lock.json`, because the reader recognises their own listing in it — recorded nowhere, so the third table inherits a rule by coin flip. And the containment check, which stops an `includes` pin on a shorter row being satisfied by a longer one, existed on the second table only, for no reason beyond the order they were written in. Both now go through one `assertPhraseTable(table, rules)` in a new `__tests__/helpers/phrase-table.ts`: the punctuation rule is a parameter named after its reason (`"never"` / `"not-at-the-end"`), the template each row is dropped into is quoted back in every failure message, and distinctness and containment come free — so a table that wants to differ has to say which rule and roughly why. (3) The clause's tail — "this refusal reads exactly like one for a checkout with no `node_modules` at all" — was written for the absent-install message and carried into the global-folder one by the same interpolation, where the reader can disprove it in the paragraph directly above: that message opens by naming the path `$NODE_PATH` answered with, and reads nothing like the absent case. A message that makes a checkable claim and gets it wrong costs the rest of the message its credit, which is more than the sentence was buying. `emptyLinkClause(root, host)` now takes a closed `EmptyLinkHost`, and the two readings live in an exported `EMPTY_LINK_TAIL` under the same table rules as the others — the global-folder row says the global answer won by default rather than on merit, which is what the finding actually means there. 12 new cases (the 9 predicate rows, both refusals asserted to carry their own tail AND to stay clear of the other's, and the tail table's own two rows). Mutation-checked three ways: restoring the shared tail turns 2 cases red, narrowing the predicate to plain names turns the scoped fixture and the table red, and a tail row that punctuates itself is caught by the shared rulebook. `npm run verify` green: typecheck + lint:all 13/13 + 5419 tests (+9) + build. ## The install directory that is not empty and still holds nothing (2026-08-16)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md: the third and fifth entries of "Empty-link follow-ups", filed by the run directly below — `readdirSync().length` misses the bookkeeping-only directory, and the clause's wording was prose the cases retyped. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-16] [est: 7000] **Count packages rather than entries, and put the empty-link wording in the table the cases read** — the finding shipped an hour earlier asked `readdirSync(...).length === 0`, and that is not the shape an interrupted install usually leaves: npm writes `.package-lock.json` BEFORE it restores the store, and a prune leaves `.bin` and `.cache` behind, so the common ending is a `node_modules` that is not empty by any count and still holds nothing node can load. The probe now reports two kinds — `empty` and `no-packages` — split on whether any entry is UNDOTTED, which is node's own line rather than a guess: a package directory is the specifier, and every specifier node resolves from here is a plain name or a `@scope`, never a leading dot. The scoped case is the one the rule has to survive and it gets its own negative, because "no plain names" and "no undotted names" agree on every fixture except a workspace whose only installed dependencies are scoped — which would be told it holds no packages at all. The second half is the treatment `OBSTRUCTION_PHRASE` got one commit earlier, applied to the clause that was written since: the wording moved into an exported `EMPTY_LINK_PHRASE`, and all four existing cases now read the phrase from there instead of retyping `/is empty/` — which was the failure that extraction exists to stop, since the two NEGATIVE cases assert an absence and a reworded clause would have made them vacuous rather than red. Its table rows are pinned the same way (verb phrase, no self-punctuation, distinct), plus one the obstruction table did not need: no phrase may CONTAIN another, or an `includes` pin on the shorter is satisfied by the longer. 5 new cases (bookkeeping-only in two shapes, the scoped package, the three table rows). No DB migration, no creds.

## The `node_modules` that is there and holds nothing (2026-08-16)

<!-- Promoted from .tasks/.suggestions.md: the second entry of "Obstruction-reporting follow-ups" — the walk asks only whether `tsx` is under each chain link, so an empty one produces no finding at all. -->

- [DONE: 2026-08-16] [est: 6000] **Name the empty `node_modules`, instead of printing the refusal for a checkout that has none** — an interrupted `npm ci`, a pruned store and a cleaned cache all leave the directory behind with nothing in it, which is the most common real version of "the install is not where you think", and it was the one shape the chain walk could not see: the walk asks only whether `tsx` is under each link, and an empty directory answers that with the same silence as a healthy one that lost a single package. So the reader got the absent-install refusal BYTE FOR BYTE — sent looking for a `node_modules` that is sitting right there in their own listing, which is the same contradiction the file-in-the-way finding was added to remove. `nearestEmptyLink` is one `readdirSync` on the nearest chain link, and the three ways it can decline are each a decision: an absent or obstructed link has `real === null` and is somebody else's finding; a `readdirSync` that THROWS is the unreadable-link finding, which already owns that path and that sentence, so answering here too would name one path twice with two explanations; and only the nearest link is asked, because an ancestor's empty `node_modules` is another project's business and one line per ancestor is the noise this classification keeps refusing to make. It gets its own clause rather than a row in `OBSTRUCTION_PHRASE` because the two end in opposite advice — something standing in a chain path must be REMOVED before anything can be installed there, and an empty directory is precisely what `npm ci` fills, so folding it into that table would tell the reader to delete the one directory they must keep. Appended to the absent-install and global-folder refusals, and deliberately not to the unreadable-link one, whose whole claim is that the question could not be decided about that very path. 4 new cases: the finding itself, the same finding carried into the global-folder message (an empty local install plus a loadable `$NODE_PATH` resolves, so that is the refusal a reader actually gets), a populated `node_modules` staying silent, and the two refusals this exists to separate — absent versus empty — required to differ with the roots normalised out. Mutation-checked both ways: dropping the emptiness test turns the populated case red, and suppressing the finding turns the other three red. `npm run verify` green: typecheck + lint:all 13/13 + 5405 tests (+4) + build. No DB migration, no creds.
